# Codex conversation 01a0f4dc-9dd3-7451-b3fa-ef81e994e2a7

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

## developer

<multi_agent_mode>Any earlier instruction enabling proactive multi-agent delegation no longer applies. Do not spawn sub-agents unless the user or applicable AGENTS.md/skill instructions explicitly ask for sub-agents, delegation, or parallel agent work.</multi_agent_mode>

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 21155)\nTotal output lines: 3\n\n{"i":0,"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"f04263","wall_time_seconds":2.7821195,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":13598,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that\'s the rule.” |\\r\\n| Inviting attention | let\'s look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |\\r\\n| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |\\r\\n| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |\\r\\n| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the …11155 tokens truncated…across assignments can leave a student with no way forward, only a link back to the same page.\\\\n- **Contradictory explanations:** several worked explanations open with \\\\\\"Yes.\\\\\\" or \\\\\\"No.\\\\\\" that contradicts the displayed answer.\\\\n- **Phone diagrams:** the arrow turns vertical on phones, but the copy and buttons still say left and right.\\\\n- **Lower-severity issues:** visual and copy problems, listed in the findings.\\\\n\\\\nVisual quality is good but does not yet reach the stated premium bar. Three of the four heroes share one composition with an orbit graphic, and on phones that graphic is cut off.\\",\\r\\n    \\"findings\\": [\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"id\\": \\"CI3-1\\",\\r\\n        \\"severity\\": \\"medium\\",\\r\\n        \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/routes.tsx\\",\\r\\n        \\"evidence\\": \\"Run keys are shared across assignments: QuestionRunner runKey is `practice:${difficulty}:${unitId ?? area ?? phase}` and `flashcards:${unitId ?? area ?? \'conditional\'}:${level}`, with no assignment ID. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The complete source progression and depth\\r\\n\\r\\nThis map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5. Notation is introduced as a solution to varied wording\\r\\n\\r\\n**How to Diagram Conditionals**, lines 289 to 388, recovers the indicator families, explains the arrow, gives a four-step construction procedure, and builds one diagram in stages. It then explains negation, displays four original/contrapositive sign patterns, gives the shortcut, and distinguishes writing a contrapositive from reading one when needed.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first diagram is narrated: notice the indicator, place its target, then complete the other side. Its final appearance is not used as evidence that the learner understands how to make it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** show each operation and its reason. Preserve Joshua\'s method and meaningful labels rather than copying the source\'s four-step count or abbreviation rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 6. Practical objections receive substantial answers\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ**, lines 390 to 433, has five questions:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Reader question | Depth of the answer | Reusable teaching value |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| When should I diagram? | Three paragraphs distinguish early training from later judgment, discuss difficult wording and chains, and address avoidance caused by uncertainty. | Give a criterion, a reason, and an honest account of the learning process. |\\r\\n| How do I know the diagram is correct? | Three paragraphs connect confidence with checked work and studying specific errors. | Teach a verification habit instead of offering confidence as a substitute for one. |\\r\\n| Does diagramming take too long? | Two paragraphs explain the proposed tradeoff and separate learning from speed. | Acknowledge the concern; explain why the slower practice step is useful now. |\\r\\n| Should I always write the contrapositive? | Two paragraphs qualify the advice. A separate note explains why the drills still require it. | Distinguish training evidence from the eventual performance habit. |\\r\\n| How should I abbreviate? | Gives a short example, a complete diagram, and advice about compact labels. | Make the notation choice purposeful and readable. Joshua\'s label guidance governs where it differs. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is unusually important to the overall tone. The writer gives practical uncertainty enough room to be answered. It is not squeezed into one generic reassurance.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 7. A useful shortcut is shown, then limited\\r\\n\\r\\n**Figuring Out The Language**, lines 437 to 471, defines an indicator\'s target and brackets the target in familiar sentences. It first shows the common placement immediately after the indicator. Then it gives five examples in which the target comes before the indicator, followed by three paired wording variations showing that the same indicator can switch position.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sequence deliberately prevents an attractive mechanical generalization. The reader sees why adjacency often helps and why it cannot replace reading the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** teach the indicator\'s complete target. Change clause order while holding meaning steady. Include one placement trap only after the usual case is understood. Mark the obscured final line as missing evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 8. A translation is checked by trying both interpretations\\r\\n\\r\\n**Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then**, lines 475 to 508, defines the check, explains its purpose, gives four steps, establishes the ordinary meaning of a new sentence, and works through a correct guess. It then deliberately tries the reversed guess and explains why that meaning fails.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis adds a verification strategy after notation. It also models the experience of being uncertain without abandoning the task. The teacher does not merely say “use your intuition”; the reader sees how to test an interpretation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** use the imagined situation as a meaning check. Preserve who or what the sentence concerns. Personalization is optional if pretending to be an object makes the wording less natural.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 9. Practice changes the language while keeping the skill\\r\\n\\r\\n**Basic Conditional Drill**, lines 512 to 563, gives supportive instructions and six themed items, with original and contrapositive diagrams displayed in the supplied HTML. The task is the same across varied sentence forms. The source says the adapted sentences come from Shakespeare and points to an answer key not included here.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** original practice can have a memorable theme, but it should change only what the learner is ready to handle. Reuse the skill, not the source\'s exercise wording. Show answers after an attempt and explain why each diagram preserves the complete ideas. Do not treat the displayed annotations as an independently verified answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe six items do not cover every expression in the source\'s own indicator tables. In particular, they do not independently train only, only if, depends, or most other necessary expressions. Their displayed diagrams also lack step-by-step explanations. Our Group 1/2 practice needs broader expression coverage and reasoned feedback; the source\'s basic set alone would not establish that coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp\\r\\n\\r\\nThe immediate authoring scope remains **what conditionals are, why they matter, sufficient, necessary, Group 1, and Group 2**. The detailed source is a quality reference. Its later topics do not automatically enter the opening.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |\\r\\n| **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |\\r\\n| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |\\r\\n| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\\r\\n| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\\r\\n| **Group 1** | Joshua\'s Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\\r\\n| **Group 2** | Joshua\'s Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression\'s target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua\'s assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give an idea several teachable moments\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve the real indicator groups\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current reviewed opening materials use:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n\\r\\nSee the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source\'s two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua\'s notes and scope.\\r\\n\\r\\n“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve Joshua\'s five steps\\r\\n\\r\\nIn [Sandra\'s 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua\'s five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Carry these improvements forward\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\\r\\n2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\\r\\n3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\\r\\n4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\\r\\n5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\\r\\n6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\\r\\n7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\\r\\n8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\\r\\n9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\\r\\n10. **Keep later content in its proper lesson.** Contraposition, chains, Groups 3/4, and quantities receive their own development; a quality reference does not override prerequisites.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\\r\\n| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\\r\\n| The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\\r\\n| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\\r\\n| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\\r\\n| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua\'s clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\\r\\n| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\\r\\n| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\\r\\n| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence\'s subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\\r\\n| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\\r\\n| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author\'s advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |\\r\\n| References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, o…21754 tokens truncated…erivatives of NotebookLM cards, 2026-10-02. See the vault asset\\r\\n// manifest and REVIEW.md for source IDs, raw originals and corrections.\\r\\nconst cards: Record<string, Card[]> = {\\r\\n  orientation: [\\r\\n    { question: \'What does a conditional statement tell you?\', answer: \'If one condition is true, the other has to be true too. The statement gives you that connection; it does not establish that either condition actually occurs. A matching fact can let you use the rule for a particular case.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 12 is marked reserved. What do we know?\', answer: \'Locker 12 has a name card. The rule covers every reserved locker, and the fact tells us Locker 12 belongs to that group. That matching condition is why the conclusion follows.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 9 has a name card. Is it necessarily marked reserved?\', answer: \'No. The sentence tells us what every reserved locker has. It has not restricted name cards to reserved lockers. Locker 9 could be reserved or unreserved; its name card alone leaves that question open.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Why do conditionals matter when evaluating an argument?\', answer: \'They can supply the connection that makes a conclusion follow. They can also show where an author needs more information. If the argument uses a rule backward, check whether anything actually gave it that additional direction.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'sufficient-necessary\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'What does sufficient mean?\', answer: \'Enough to establish the other condition under this rule. Once the sufficient condition is met, the rule gives you the necessary condition. Enough does not mean it is the only possible way for the other condition to be true.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every order marked ready has been packed. Does packing establish the ready label?\', answer: \\"No. Every ready order has to be packed, but the sentence doesn\'t say every packed order is marked ready. A packed order without a ready label fits the rule. We need more information to settle its ready status.\\" },\\r\\n    { question: \'What does necessary mean?\', answer: \'Required whenever the sufficient condition holds. Ask required for what, and read the complete relationship. In Marked ready → Packed, packing is required for every ready order; those roles belong to this particular rule.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'If a requirement is met, does that prove the other condition happened?\', answer: \'No. A requirement may be present while the other condition remains unknown. Entry requires a pass, but a person could have a pass and stay outside. The rule has not turned the pass into a guarantee of entry.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'group-1\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'A student receives feedback if the student revises the essay. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Revises essay → Receives feedback. If refers to revising, even though that clause appears last. The sentence gives feedback to each student who revises. Feedback alone does not establish that the student revised.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. Why is “Applicant → Pays fee” too broad?\', answer: \'It drops the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would include someone who submitted on time, whom the sentence does not cover. Keep the complete rule: Applicant submits late → Pays fee.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every student who registers early receives a discount. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Student registers early → Receives discount. Every refers to students who register early. Early registration is part of the sufficient condition, so keep it when you shorten the idea.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A fee is paid by any applicant who submits late. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Applicant submits late → Pays fee. Any still refers to late-submitting applicants. Moving that phrase later does not change its sufficient role. Read the arrow as: if an applicant submits late, that applicant pays a fee.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'group-2\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'A permit is required for construction. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Construction → Has permit. What is required? The permit, even though it appears before required. The sentence makes it necessary for construction, not enough to establish that construction occurs.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Why not automatically take the words immediately after the indicator?\', answer: \'The complete target can appear before or after the expression. In a permit is required for construction, the permit is the requirement. Ask what the indicator refers to, then check your diagram against the meaning of the whole sentence.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A finalist must attend the interview. Maya attended. Does that prove she is a finalist?\', answer: \'No. The rule gives finalists an obligation; it does not say only finalists attend. Actual attendance is also different from being required to attend. The fact about Maya does not establish finalist status.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A garden light switches on only when its sensor detects darkness. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Light switches on → Sensor detects darkness. Only when makes darkness detection required for switching on. The sentence has not said detection is enough, so knowing the sensor detects darkness alone would leave the light unknown.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Recall({ items }: { items: Card[] }) {\\r\\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const answerId = useId();\\r\\n  const item = items[index];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-flash\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Review · {index + 1} of {items.length}</p>\\r\\n    <div className={`co-flash-face ${revealed ? \'co-flipped\' : \'\'}`}>\\r\\n      <strong>{item.question}</strong><span>Answer in your own words, then compare.</span>\\r\\n      <div id={answerId} aria-live=\\"polite\\">{revealed && <p>{item.answer}</p>}</div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\">\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? \'Hide recall explanation\' : \'Reveal recall explanation\'}</button>\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setRevealed(false); }}>Previous example</button>\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setRevealed(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? \'Start these again\' : \'Next example\'}</button>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalOpeningMedia({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {\\r\\n  const items = cards[unitId];\\r\\n  if (!items) return null;\\r\\n  return <section id=\\"co-return-to-the-relationship\\" className=\\"co-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Return to the relationship\\">\\r\\n    <h2>Return to the relationship</h2>\\r\\n    <p>Try these before moving on. Say what the rule gives you and explain why.</p>\\r\\n    <Recall key={unitId} items={items}/>\\r\\n    {unitId === \'sufficient-necessary\' && <details className=\\"co-media-summary\\">\\r\\n      <summary>See the relationships together</summary>\\r\\n      <figure className=\\"co-relationship-overview\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Every order marked ready has been packed.</h3>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-overview-roles\\"><div><strong>Marked ready</strong><span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</b><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">implies</span><div><strong>Packed</strong><span>Necessary: required</span></div></div>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-overview-set\\">\\r\\n          <svg viewBox=\\"0 0 600 250\\" role=\\"img\\" aria-labelledby=\\"co-order-overview-title co-order-overview-desc\\">\\r\\n            <title id=\\"co-order-overview-title\\">Marked ready is sufficient. Packed is necessary.</title>\\r\\n            <desc id=\\"co-order-overview-desc\\">The ready group is contained within packed orders. All ready orders are packed. Packed alone does not establish the ready label. The drawing does not require unequal group sizes.</desc>\\r\\n            <rect x=\\"10\\" y=\\"10\\" width=\\"580\\" height=\\"230\\" rx=\\"24\\" fill=\\"#d7e9df\\"/>\\r\\n            <rect x=\\"40\\" y=\\"78\\" width=\\"285\\" height=\\"132\\" rx=\\"18\\" fill=\\"#fcf3df\\" stroke=\\"#bd8a47\\"/>\\r\\n          </svg>\\r\\n          <span className=\\"co-overview-packed-label\\">Packed orders</span><span className=\\"co-overview-ready-label\\">Orders marked ready</span>\\r\\n        </div>\\r\\n        <p>Packed alone: the ready label remains unknown.</p>\\r\\n        <figcaption>Read the arrow in its stated direction. An order could be packed without a ready label.</figcaption>\\r\\n      </figure>\\r\\n    </details>}\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}}\n{"i":3,"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"83cf26","wall_time_seconds":5.9874410000000005,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2773,"output":".co-opening{--co-ink:#162d32;--co-teal:#136b66;--co-paper:#faf8f0;--co-coral:#c65939;max-width:1060px;background:var(--co-paper);color:var(--co-ink);padding:0;border-radius:24px;overflow:hidden}.co-opening [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'import { useId, useState, type ReactNode } from \'react\';\r\nimport { Link } from \'react-router-dom\';\r\nimport { DynamicIslandTOC } from \'@/components/ui/dynamic-island-toc\';\r\nimport { GROUPS } from \'@/features/conditional-refresher/content\';\r\nimport type { ConditionalLessonNoteProps } from \'./ConditionalLessonNote\';\r\nimport \'./opening.css\';\r\nimport { ConditionalOpeningMedia } from \'./ConditionalOpeningMedia\';\r\n\r\nexport const hasOpeningLesson = (id: string) => [\'orientation\', \'sufficient-necessary\', \'group-1\', \'group-2\'].includes(id);\r\nfunction Section({ title, children, dark = false, expressionNumber, id }: { title: string; children: ReactNode; dark?: boolean; expressionNumber?: number; id?: string }) {\r\n  return <section id={id || `co-${title.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, \'-\')}`} className={`co-section ${dark ? \'co-dark\' : \'\'} ${expressionNumber ? \'co-expression-section\' : \'\'}`} data-toc data-toc-title={title}>\r\n    <div className="co-section-heading">{expressionNumber && <span className="co-expression-number" aria-hidden="true">{String(expressionNumber).padStart(2, \'0\')}</span>}<h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}\r\n  </section>;\r\n}\r\nfunction Arrow({ left, right }: { left: string; right: string }) {\r\n  return <div className="co-arrow"><span>{left}</span><b aria-hidden="true">→</b><span className="co-sr-only">implies</span><span>{right}</span></div>;\r\n}\r\nfunction ThinkPause({ prompt, children }: { prompt: string; children: ReactNode }) {\r\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\r\n  const answerId = useId();\r\n  return <div className="co-think-pause"><p className="co-kicker">Pause here</p><h3>{prompt}</h3><p>Think it through before revealing. Say what you know and why.</p><button type="button" className="co-primary" aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? \'Hide the reasoning\' : \'Show the reasoning\'}</button><div id={answerId} aria-live="polite">{revealed && <div className="co-pause-answer">{children}</div>}</div></div>;\r\n}\r\nfunction Check({ prompt, options, correct, feedback }: { prompt: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] }) {\r\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<number | null>(null);\r\n  if (!Array.isArray(feedback) || feedback.length !== options.length || feedback.some(message => typeof message !== \'string\' || !message.trim())) {\r\n    throw new Error(`Every answer choice needs an explanation: ${prompt}`);\r\n  }\r\n  return <div className="co-check"><p className="co-kicker">Try it</p><h3>{prompt}</h3><div className="co-choices">{options.map((option, i) => <button key={option} type="button" aria-pressed={choice === i} onClick={() => setChoice(i)}>{option}</button>)}</div><div role="status">{choice !== null && <div className={`co-feedback ${choice === correct ? \'co-correct\' : \'\'}`} ><strong>{choice === correct ? \'Yes.\' : `Your choice: ${options[choice]}`}</strong><p>{feedback[choice]}</p></div>}</div></div>;\r\n}\r\nfunction SetExplorer() {\r\n  const [fact, setFact] = useState<\'ready\' | \'packed\'>(\'ready\');\r\n  const [placed, setPlaced] = useState(false);\r\n  const [possibility, setPossibility] = useState<\'ready\' | \'not-ready\'>(\'ready\');\r\n  const unknown = fact === \'packed\';\r\n  const x = !placed ? 460 : !unknown || possibility === \'ready\' ? 183 : 460;\r\n  return <div className="co-lab"><div className="co-lab-heading"><p className="co-kicker">One rule. Two starting facts.</p><h3>Every order marked ready has been packed.</h3></div>\r\n    <svg viewBox="0 0 640 330" className="co-set" role="img" aria-label={placed ? unknown ? `Order B is packed. Its ready status is unknown. Showing an allowed ${possibility === \'ready\' ? \'ready\' : \'not ready\'} placement.` : \'Order A is marked ready and is therefore packed.\' : \'Packed orders contain every order marked ready. Choose a fact to explore.\'}>\r\n      <rect x="28" y="25" width="584" height="275" rx="28" className="co-packed"/><text x="54" y="63">Packed orders</text><rect x="70" y="99" width="240" height="164" rx="24" className="co-ready"/><text x="94" y="131">Marked ready</text>{placed && <g className="co-animal" style={{ transform: `translate(${x}px,${placed ? 198 : 125}px)` }}><circle r="35"/><text textAnchor="middle" y="6">Order {unknown ? \'B\' : \'A\'}</text></g>}\r\n    </svg><div className="co-choices"><button aria-pressed={fact === \'ready\'} onClick={() => { setFact(\'ready\'); setPlaced(false); }}>Order A is marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={fact === \'packed\'} onClick={() => { setFact(\'packed\'); setPlaced(false); setPossibility(\'ready\'); }}>Order B has been packed</button><button onClick={() => setPlaced(true)}>Place the order</button></div>\r\n    {placed && unknown && <div className="co-choices" role="group" aria-label="Allowed placements, not established facts"><button aria-pressed={possibility === \'ready\'} onClick={() => setPossibility(\'ready\')}>Could be marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={possibility === \'not-ready\'} onClick={() => setPossibility(\'not-ready\')}>Could be packed without a ready label</button></div>}\r\n    <p className="co-feedback" role="status">{!placed ? \'Choose a fact, then use the rule to see what we know.\' : !unknown ? \'Order A is marked ready, so it must have been packed. The ready label gives us packing.\' : \'Order B has been packed. Whether it is marked ready remains unknown. Both placements fit the rule; neither has been established.\'}</p><p className="co-small">The rule requires every ready order to be packed. It does not require more packed orders than ready orders. This drawing shows an allowed arrangement.</p></div>;\r\n}\r\nfunction GateExplorer() {\r\n  const [fact, setFact] = useState<\'ready\' | \'packed\'>(\'ready\');\r\n  return <div className="co-gate"><p className="co-kicker">Enough and required</p><Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/><div className="co-choices"><button aria-pressed={fact === \'ready\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'ready\')}>Order A is marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={fact === \'packed\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'packed\')}>Order B has been packed</button></div><div className="co-gate-track"><div className={fact === \'ready\' ? \'co-lit\' : \'\'}>Marked ready<span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden="true">→</b><span className="co-sr-only">implies</span><div className="co-lit">Packed<span>Necessary: required</span></div></div><p role="status">{fact === \'ready\' ? \'We know Order A has been packed because every ready order is packed.\' : \'We know Order B has been packed. Its ready status remains unknown. An order can be packed without a ready label.\'}</p></div>;\r\n}\r\ntype Translation = { word: string; sentence: string; target: string; other: string; left: string; right: string; why: string; readback: string };\r\ntype DiagramTranslation = Translation & { reversedPromise: string };\r\n\r\nexport function orderedConditionalIdeas(item: Pick<Translation, \'sentence\' | \'target\' | \'other\'>): [string, string] {\r\n  let hash = 2166136261;\r\n  for (const character of item.sentence) hash = Math.imul(hash ^ character.charCodeAt(0), 16777619);\r\n  const reverseOrder = ((hash ^ (hash >>> 16)) >>> 0) % 2 === 1;\r\n  return reverseOrder ? [item.other, item.target] : [item.target, item.other];\r\n}\r\n\r\nexport const translationExamples: Record<string, Translation[]> = {\r\n  \'group-1\': [\r\n    { word: \'if\', sentence: \'A report is reviewed if it is submitted before noon.\', target: \'Submitted before noon\', other: \'Reviewed\', left: \'Submitted before noon\', right: \'Reviewed\', why: \'If what?
