It doesn\'t establish compliance. Keep required to wear helmet in the diagram so the stated obligation remains an obligation."\r\n    }\r\n  ]\r\n};\r\nfunction TargetSentence({ item, marked }: { item: MappedTranslation; marked: boolean }) {\r\n  const figure = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);\r\n  const indicator = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);\r\n  const target = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);\r\n  const [connector, setConnector] = useState<string | null>(null);\r\n  const indicatorStart = item.sentence.toLowerCase().indexOf(item.word.toLowerCase());\r\n  const targetStart = item.sentence.indexOf(item.sentenceTarget);\r\n  if (indicatorStart < 0 || targetStart < 0) throw new Error(`Missing authored sentence span: ${item.sentence}`);\r\n  const spans = [\r\n    { start: indicatorStart, end: indicatorStart + item.word.length, kind: \'indicator\' },\r\n    { start: targetStart, end: targetStart + item.sentenceTarget.length, kind: \'target\' },\r\n  ].sort((a, b) => a.start - b.start);\r\n  if (spans[0].end > spans[1].start) throw new Error(`Overlapping authored sentence spans: ${item.sentence}`);\r\n\r\n  useLayoutEffect(() => {\r\n    if (!marked) { setConnector(null); return; }\r\n    const update = () => {\r\n      if (!figure.current || !indicator.current || !target.current) return;\r\n      const origin = figure.current.getBoundingClientRect();\r\n      const firstRects = indicator.current.getClientRects();\r\n      const targetRects = target.current.getClientRects();\r\n      const first = firstRects[firstRects.length - 1];\r\n      const last = targetRects[targetRects.length - 1];\r\n      if (!first?.width || !last?.width) return;\r\n      const x1 = (first.left + first.right) / 2 - origin.left;\r\n      const x2 = (last.left + last.right) / 2 - origin.left;\r\n      const y1 = first.bottom - origin.top + 5;\r\n      const y2 = last.bottom - origin.top + 5;\r\n      const baseline = Math.max(y1, y2) + 28;\r\n      setConnector(`M ${x1} ${y1} V ${baseline} H ${x2} V ${y2}`);\r\n    };\r\n    update();\r\n    const observer = typeof ResizeObserver === \'undefined\' ? null : new ResizeObserver(update);\r\n    if (figure.current) observer?.observe(figure.current);\r\n    window.addEventListener(\'resize\', update);\r\n    return () => { observer?.disconnect(); window.removeEventListener(\'resize\', update); };\r\n  }, [item.sentence, marked]);\r\n\r\n  if (!marked) return <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>;\r\n  return <figure className="co-target-sentence" ref={figure}>\r\n    <blockquote>{item.sentence.slice(0, spans[0].start)}\r\n      <mark className={`co-sentence-${spans[0].kind}`} ref={spans[0].kind === \'indicator\' ? indicator : target}>{item.sentence.slice(spans[0].start, spans[0].end)}</mark>\r\n      {item.sentence.slice(spans[0].end, spans[1].start)}\r\n      <mark className={`co-sentence-${spans[1].kind}`} ref={spans[1].kind === \'indicator\' ? indicator : target}>{item.sentence.slice(spans[1].start, spans[1].end)}</mark>\r\n      {item.sentence.slice(spans[1].end)}\r\n    </blockquote>\r\n    {connector && <svg className="co-target-connector" aria-hidden="true"><path d={connector}/></svg>}\r\n    <figcaption><span>Expression: <strong>{item.word}</strong></span><span>Complete target: <strong>{item.sentenceTarget}</strong></span></figcaption>\r\n  </figure>;\r\n}\r\n\r\nfunction TranslationLab({ id }: { id: string }) {\r\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\r\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<string | null>(null);\r\n  const [diagram, setDiagram] = useState(false);\r\n  const item = translationExamples[id][index];\r\n  return <div className="co-lab"><p className="co-kicker">Read the whole idea</p><div className="co-word-tabs" role="group" aria-label="Indicator examples">{translationExamples[id].map((e, i) => <button key={e.word} aria-pressed={i === index} onClick={() => { setIndex(i); setChoice(null); setDiagram(false); }}>{e.word}</button>)}</div><TargetSentence item={item} marked={choice === item.target}/><p>What is <strong>{item.word}</strong> referring to?</p><div className="co-choices">{orderedConditionalIdeas(item).map(value => <button key={value} aria-pressed={choice === value} onClick={() => { setChoice(value); setDiagram(false); }}>{value}</button>)}</div><div role="status">{choice && <div className="co-feedback"><strong>{choice === item.target ? \'You have the complete target.\' : \'You chose the other condition.\'}</strong>{choice !== item.target && <p>You chose {choice}, but {item.word} refers to {item.target}. {id === \'group-1\' ? \'You selected the result rather than the complete sufficient idea.\' : \'You selected the condition that needs the requirement rather than the required idea.\'}</p>}<p>{item.why}</p>{choice === item.target && <button className="co-primary" onClick={() => setDiagram(true)}>Place as the {id === \'group-1\' ? \'sufficient\' : \'required\'} side</button>}</div>}</div>{diagram && <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>}</div>;\r\n}\r\nfunction Flashcards({ id }: { id: string }) {\r\n  const cards = flashcardExamples[id];\r\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\r\n  const [back, setBack] = useState(false);\r\n  const faceId = useId();\r\n  const card = cards[index];\r\n'}]

In orientation\'s first formative check (line 781), the prompt is \'Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?\' and the correct option is \'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.\' A student who answers correctly sees a bold \'Yes.\' and then \'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown.\' This is the same contradictory bare Yes/No that CI3-2 removed from drills. It appears at the learner\'s first decision in the course.",\r\n        "fix": "Replace the fixed \'Yes.\' with role-neutral confirmation copy, for example \'Your choice: {option}\' styled as correct, or \'That follows.\' Alternatively, let each Check pass an optional correct-state heading. The orientation lesson the student actually reads (Fundamentals, ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx lines 734-784) teaches guarantee vs tendency, rule vs case fact, direction and possibility. It never discusses permission words or instructions versus completed actions. Obligation is first taught in Group 2\'s Must section, and \'may\' appears only in unrendered source teaching. Teacher-assigned advanced orientation therefore assesses an untaught operation. That breaks the plan\'s rule that each assessed operation is taught first and the guide\'s progression rubric.",\r\n        "fix": "Keep the question IDs and meanings frozen. In the \'A guarantee is stronger than a tendency\' section, add a short paragraph and pause, for example: \'Keep the rule\'s own strength. A rule that says someone may do something gives permission, not a report that they did it. An instruction tells you what should happen, not what happened.\' Add one formative Check with option-specific feedback. The guide says to \'be certain about the limits of what is known\', and CI3-6 fixed the same \'may not\' pattern in orientation. Separately, line 593 reads \'Every finalist receives an invitation, so finalist status settles that question.\' It answers a pause about whether an invitation proves finalist status, so \'that question\' points to the wrong question.",\r\n        "fix": "Use firm wording: \'Membership by itself leaves admission unknown.\' / \'Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\' Rewrite line 593 as, for example: \'The rule starts from finalists. It tells you what each finalist gets, not who else might get an invitation.\'"\r\n      },\r\n      {\r\n        "id": "FI4-4",\r\n        "severity": "low",\r\n        "path": "src/bootcamps/conditionals/routes.tsx",\r\n        "evidence": "UnitActions links \'4. Nothing in src/bootcamps/conditionals or the TOC reads `location.hash` or calls scrollIntoView. The lesson also waits for progress loading in Shell. So in-app navigation lands at the previous or top scroll position, not at #repair. The section#repair anchors now exist and are tested. The \'repair navigation landing at 80px\' browser record came from the DEV harness or a direct load, which has no UnitActions link. This limitation predates the revision, but it undercuts the claim of \'actual repair anchors\' on the real route.",\r\n        "fix": "Add a small effect in Lesson (or ConditionalOpeningLesson) that waits until the lesson renders, then calls `document.getElementById(location.hash.slice(1))?.scrollIntoView()` when the hash is present. Add a route test that navigates via the \'Review the move\' link and asserts scrollIntoView was called on #repair."\r\n      },\r\n      {\r\n        "id": "FI4-5",\r\n        "severity": "low",\r\n        "path": "src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/opening.css",\r\n        "evidence": "The CI3-4 change removed the looping orbit from the TSX. opening.css still contains `.co-hero-orbit` rules, `animation:co-flow 3s ... infinite` and the paused-state rule. The hero now has no motion, and the toggle only disables the order-circle and flashcard transitions far below. A control that appears to do nothing where it sits is confusing, and the dead CSS is misleading.",\r\n        "fix": "Delete the orbit and keyframe CSS. Move the motion toggle next to the SetExplorer and flashcards it actually controls, or relabel it \'Reduce animation in this lesson\' in the footer or near the TOC. Keep the prefers-reduced-motion handling."\r\n      },\r\n      {\r\n        "id": "FI4-6",\r\n        "severity": "low",\r\n        "path": "src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx",\r\n        "evidence": "The orientation hero figure (lines 914-919) and the first section\'s `.co-intro-relationship` block (line 739) show the same two panels, \'If this is true...\' / \'...this has to be true too.\' They are one scroll apart. The guide says repetition must change the reader\'s view and not render the same thing twice in different panels. The guide principles held up well against the copy:\\n- \'Define before demonstrating\' and \'relationship comes before its parts\': orientation opens \'A condition is something that can be true or false…\', explains why direction matters on the LSAT, then gives the anchor \'Every order marked ready has been packed\', and only then the arrow (\'Now that we understand the sentence, we can give the two conditions shorter labels\').\\n- \'Confidence lives in the reasoning / We don\'t know\': \'We don\'t know. Order B could be marked ready. It could also be packed but not marked ready. Both possibilities fit the statement.\'\\n- \'Prediction before a reveal\' and the I-hypothetical pronoun role: separate ThinkPauses \'If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?\' / \'If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?\'\\n- \'Make feedback identify the missed connection\': the reversals name concrete counterexamples (\'A guardrail beside a gentle slope would fit the sentence too. The template is clear, but its cadence becomes predictable by expression 5. Material voice issues are FI4-1 and FI4-3; the rest is polish.",\r\n      "(b) LEARNING PROGRESSION AND DEPTH:\\n- Order: meaning → purpose → tendency vs guarantee → anchor → case fact → arrow → changed fact → containment visual → transfer check.\\n- Sufficient and necessary get separate beats on the same rule: \'Enough does not mean the only way\' and \'Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\'. Personalized If/Then gets paired pauses in both directions.\\n- Each group has seven expression subsections with clause-order variants, inquiry \'asked if\', a one-occasion \'when\', only vs the only on the ready/packed rule, and must as obligation. The What Test is taught before the target lab. opening drills and found every key logically correct.\\n- Gap: orientation advanced items test permission/instruction strength without a taught beat (FI4-2).\\n- Structural comparis…1930 tokens strokeWidth) / 2;\r\n  const circumference = 2 * Math.PI * radius;\r\n  const offset = circumference - (percentage / 100) * circumference;\r\n\r\n  return (\r\n    <svg width={size} height={size} className="-rotate-90 shrink-0">\r\n      <circle cx={size / 2} cy={size / 2} r={radius} fill="none" stroke="var(--muted)" strokeWidth={strokeWidth} />\r\n      <motion.circle\r\n        cx={size / 2}\r\n        cy={size / 2}\r\n        r={radius}\r\n        fill="none"\r\n        stroke="var(--foreground)"\r\n        strokeWidth={strokeWidth}\r\n        strokeDasharray={circumference}\r\n        initial={{ strokeDashoffset: circumference }}\r\n        animate={{ strokeDashoffset: offset }}\r\n        transition={{ duration: 0.15, ease: "easeOut" }}\r\n        strokeLinecap="round"\r\n      />\r\n    </svg>\r\n  );\r\n}\r\n\r\n// --- Main Component ---\r\n\r\ntype DynamicIslandTOCProps = {\r\n  children?: ReactNode;\r\n  /**\r\n   * CSS selector to find headings.\r\n   * Defaults to common blog content wrappers and explicit [data-toc] elements.\r\n   */\r\n  selector?: string;\r\n};\r\n\r\nexport function DynamicIslandTOC({\r\n  children,\r\n  selector = "article h1, article h2, article h3, article h4, .prose h1, .prose h2, .prose h3, .prose h4, [data-toc]",\r\n}: DynamicIslandTOCProps) {\r\n  const [headings, setHeadings] = useState<HeadingData[]>([]);\r\n  const [activeId, setActiveId] = useState<string | null>(null);\r\n  const [hoveredId, setHoveredId] = useState<string | null>(null);\r\n  const [isExpanded, setIsExpanded] = useState(false);\r\n  const [progress, setProgress] = useState(0);\r\n\r\n  // 1. DOM Scanning Strategy\r\n  useEffect(() => {\r\n    const getHeadings = () => {\r\n      const elements = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll(selector)) as HTMLElement[];\r\n\r\n      const validHeadings = elements\r\n        .filter((el) => !el.hasAttribute("data-toc-ignore")) // Allow explicit skipping\r\n        .map((el, index) => {\r\n          // Auto-generate ID if missing (common in generic Markdown/CMS output)\r\n          if (!el.id) {\r\n            const generatedId =\r\n              el.textContent\r\n                ?.toLowerCase()\r\n                .replace(/\\s+/g, "-")\r\n                .replace(/[^\\w-]/g, "") || `toc-heading-${index}`;\r\n            el.id = generatedId;\r\n          }\r\n\r\n          // 1. Check data-toc-depth attribute\r\n          // 2. Fallback to standard HTML tag levels (H1 = 1, H2 = 2)\r\n          // 3. Default to level 2 if not a heading tag\r\n          const depthAttr = el.getAttribute("data-toc-depth");\r\n          let level = 2;\r\n\r\n          if (depthAttr) {\r\n            level = parseInt(depthAttr, 10);\r\n          } else {\r\n            const tagName = el.tagName.toUpperCase();\r\n            if (tagName.startsWith("H") && tagName.length === 2) {\r\n              level = parseInt(tagName[1], 10);\r\n            }\r\n          }\r\n\r\n          // Allow title overrides via data-toc-title\r\n          const text = el.getAttribute("data-toc-title") || el.textContent || "Section";\r\n\r\n          return { id: el.id, text, level, element: el };\r\n        });\r\n\r\n      // Sort by DOM order mathematically\r\n      validHeadings.sort((a, b) =>\r\n        a.element.compareDocumentPosition(b.element) & Node.DOCUMENT_POSITION_FOLLOWING ? -1 : 1,\r\n      );\r\n\r\n      setHeadings(validHeadings);\r\n    };\r\n\r\n    // Slight delay ensures CMS/Markdown hydration is complete\r\n    const timer = setTimeout(getHeadings, 100);\r\n    return () => clearTimeout(timer);\r\n  }, [selector]);\r\n\r\n  // 2. Scroll Spy & Progress\r\n  useEffect(() => {\r\n    const handleScroll = () => {\r\n      let currentActiveId: string | null = null;\r\n      for (const heading of headings) {\r\n        const top = heading.element.getBoundingClientRect().top;\r\n        // 120px offset to trigger active state just as heading reaches the top\r\n        if (top <= 120) {\r\n          currentActiveId = heading.id;\r\n        } else {\r\n          break;\r\n        }\r\n      }\r\n\r\n      if (!currentActiveId && headings.length > 0) {\r\n        currentActiveId = headings[0].id;\r\n      }\r\n\r\n      setActiveId(currentActiveId);\r\n\r\n      const total = document.documentElement.scrollHeight - window.innerHeight;\r\n      setProgress(total > 0 ? Math.min(100, Math.max(0, (window.scrollY / total) * 100)) : 0);\r\n    };\r\n\r\n    window.addEventListener("scroll", handleScroll, { passive: true });\r\n    handleScroll();\r\n\r\n    return () => window.removeEventListener("scroll", handleScroll);\r\n  }, [headings]);\r\n\r\n  const activeHeading = headings.find((h) => h.id === activeId);\r\n  const [isVisible, setIsVisible] = useState(false);\r\n\r\n  // Normalize depths so the highest-level heading in the doc touches the left edge\r\n  const minLevel = useMemo(() => {\r\n    if (headings.length === 0) return 1;\r\n    return Math.min(...headings.map((h) => h.level));\r\n  }, [headings]);\r\n\r\n  useEffect(() => {\r\n    const checkVisibility = () => {\r\n      // Hide if at the very top (Hero section)\r\n      setIsVisible(window.scrollY > 400);\r\n    };\r\n    window.addEventListener("scroll", checkVisibility, { passive: true });\r\n    checkVisibility();\r\n    return () => window.removeEventListener("scroll", checkVisibility);\r\n  }, []);\r\n\r\n  return (\r\n    <>\r\n      {children}\r\n\r\n      {/* Backdrop Blur Overlay */}\r\n      <AnimatePresence>\r\n        {isExpanded && (\r\n          <motion.div\r\n            initial={{ opacity: 0 }}\r\n            animate={{ opacity: 1 }}\r\n            exit={{ opacity: 0 }}\r\n            transition={islandTransition}\r\n            className="fixed inset-0 z-[9998] bg-black/20 backdrop-blur-[4px]"\r\n            onClick={() => setIsExpanded(false)}\r\n          />\r\n        )}\r\n      </AnimatePresence>\r\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '        "The sentence hasn\'t promised that every electric vehicle is admitted. An electric vehicle that remains outside would fit the rule.",\r\n      ],\r\n      after: [\r\n        "This is why the only belongs with our sufficient expressions: the complete group it introduces gives us the starting condition. Read it as a whole expression. We\'ll compare it with only once we\'ve learned the other group.",\r\n      ],\r\n    },\r\n    {\r\n      title: \'Every: each qualifying case gets the result\',\r\n      paragraphs: [\r\n        "Every gives finalist status the same sufficient job that all gave registered-guest status. Now try the reverse below.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n      <ThinkPause prompt={lesson.prompt}>\\r\\n        {lesson.reasoning.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\\r\\n        <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>\\r\\n        <p className=\\"co-readback\\">{item.readback}</p>\\r\\n      </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      {lesson.after?.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\\r\\n      {lesson.check && <Check {...lesson.check}/>}\\r\\n      {id === \'group-2\' && item.word === \'only if\' && <DirectionSwitch/>}\\r\\n    </Section>;\\r\\n  })}</>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Fundamentals({ roles = false }: { roles?: boolean }) {\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    {!roles && <>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"One condition tells you something else\\">\\r\\n        <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something that can be true or false of the situation we\'re considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. Each gives us an idea whose truth we can ask about.</p>\\r\\n        <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn\'t tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow if it did.</p>\\r\\n        <p>That distinction matters. You can understand a rule about people who have tickets without knowing whether anyone has a ticket. Later, a fact about one person may give you a way to use the rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p>You don\'t need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Why learn this?\\" dark>\\r\\n        <p>On the LSAT, you\'re often deciding whether a conclusion follows from what you\'ve been told. A conditional may supply the connection you need. It can also show you exactly where an argument asks you to accept more than its information supports.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The important part is the direction. If one condition guarantees another, can we start with the other condition and go back? Another statement might allow that. But this rule alone doesn\'t.</p>\\r\\n        <p>We\'ll work with one everyday rule and change what we know about a particular order. You\'ll see a conclusion the rule supports and another that would need more information. That distinction helps you check what must be true and decide whether an argument has gone too far.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"A guarantee is stronger than a tendency\\">\\r\\n        <p>Compare these two statements:</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-intro-relationship\\"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>Every order marked ready has been packed.</strong><p>A ready order cannot be an exception under this rule.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. You could find an order marked ready that hasn\'t been packed and still accept the first statement. The second doesn\'t leave that possibility open. Every order with that label has been packed.</p>\\r\\n        <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we\'re asking what follows from this particular statement. The rule gives us a guarantee within the information we\'re considering.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Keep the sentence\'s own strength. A rule can give you permission to do something without saying you did it. It can also tell you what you\'re supposed to do without reporting that you followed the instruction. Those are different claims.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>May use gives permission here. A guest with a ticket is allowed to use the cloakroom. That guest could leave their coat on and never go inside. The permission is still there.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Lena has a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. We know she\'s allowed to use it because the rule gives that permission to every guest with a ticket. We haven\'t been told whether she used it.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Compare this instruction: <strong>If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack.</strong> A wet coat makes the instruction call for hanging it up. That doesn\'t report anyone carrying out the instruction. We\'d need a fact about what they actually did.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\\r\\n          \\"You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn\'t reported that anyone did it.\\",\\r\\n          \\"The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.\\",\\r\\n          \\"The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn\'t say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.\\",\\r\\n        ]}/>\\r\\n        <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn\'t used, and told to return isn\'t returned.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Read one rule in ordinary English\\">\\r\\n        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>There are two complete ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready</strong>, and an order <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence tells us something about every order that meets the first condition.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Read it this way: <strong>if an order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> We don\'t need to know why the shop uses that label. We need to know what the sentence tells us about an order that has it.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The rule itself doesn\'t identify a particular ready order. A separate fact can tell us whether one of the conditions is met in a particular case.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order A is marked ready. What do we now know about it?\\">\\r\\n          <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact tells us that Order A is one of those orders. That\'s the connection. The rule tells us what has to be true of the group; the fact lets us apply it to this case.</p>\\r\\n          <p>We haven\'t learned that every order in the shop is packed. Our conclusion concerns Order A because that\'s the order the fact tells us about.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"The arrow is a shorter way to write it\\">\\r\\n        <p>Now that we understand the sentence, we can give the two conditions shorter labels. The arrow records the connection between them.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Marked ready\\" right=\\"Packed\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>Read it from left to right: <strong>if the order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> Knowing the left condition lets us establish the right because every ready order is packed.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The arrow doesn\'t mean “and then.” It also doesn\'t claim the label causes the packing. We\'re recording what follows logically, not a timeline.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Change the fact, keep the rule\\">\\r\\n        <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, the fact tells us about packing instead of the ready label.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready?\\">\\r\\n          <p><strong>We don\'t know.</strong> Order B could be marked ready. It could also be packed but not marked ready. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>\\r\\n          <p>The rule tells us about every ready order. It hasn\'t told us that every packed order gets that label. To conclude that Order B is ready, we\'d need that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>In the drawing below, ready orders sit inside packed orders because every ready order is packed. A packed order can fit inside or outside the ready group. Use the two facts to compare what each one establishes.</p>\\r\\n        <SetExplorer/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Try the same reasoning with a new rule\\">\\r\\n        <p>You\'ve used the rule in its stated direction and checked what happens when you start from its result. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?\\" options={[\'Yes, the badge proves it.\', \'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.\', \'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.\']}/>\\r\\n        <p>A matching result can make the reverse feel plausible. Check whether the sentence actually gave you that direction before accepting it.</p>\\r\\n        <p>We can now name the two jobs the conditions are doing. One is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first holds.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n    </>}\\r\\n    {roles && <>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Sufficient means enough\\">\\r\\n        <p>An order being marked ready is sufficient for it being packed. It\'s enough. Once we know the order is marked ready, this rule gives us what we need to conclude it has been packed.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>You don\'t need a second fact about its contents. The rule already tells you what must be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>\\r\\n          <p>This doesn\'t make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Enough does not mean the only way\\">\\r\\n        <p>Suppose Order D isn\'t marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn\'t settle the packing question.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order D isn\'t marked ready. Could it still have been packed?\\">\\r\\n          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn\'t been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>\\r\\n          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn\'t said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn\'t present, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?\\" options={[\'Marked collected\', \'Has a pickup receipt\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.\', \'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.\']}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Necessary means required\\">\\r\\n        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready has to have been packed. Being packed is necessary for being marked ready.</p>\\r\\n        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Which condition does a ready order have to meet?\\">\\r\\n          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we\'d have a ready order that wasn\'t packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>\\r\\n          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>\\r\\n        <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Enters the archive\\" right=\\"Has a pass\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?\\" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', \\"You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too.\\"]}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?\\">\\r\\n          <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. My order could be packed without a ready label. That fits every ready order is packed because it doesn\'t create a ready order that hasn\'t been packed.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Packed → Marked ready would add a claim about every packed order. The sentence doesn\'t give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Read the roles in the relationship\\">\\r\\n        <p>In <strong>Marked ready → Packed</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it\'s enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it\'s required whenever the left holds.</p>\\r\\n        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don\'t ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n    </>}\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === \'group-1\' ? \'g1\' : \'g2\'))!;\\r\\n  const first = id === \'group-1\';\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the sufficient idea\' : \'Find what is required\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We\'ll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-vocabulary\\">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. Finding that idea matters more than taking the next few words in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <IndicatorSections id={id}/>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the target, then place it\' : \'Find the requirement, then place it\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Add what to the indicator when that gives you a sensible question. If what? All what? The only what? Your answer should be the whole idea the expression refers to. Root element is missing.\\r\\nInvalidOperation: Index operation failed; the array index evaluated to null.\\r\\nInvalidOperation: Index operation failed; the array index evaluated to null.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that\'s the rule.” |\\r\\n| Inviting attention | let\'s look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |\\r\\n| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |\\r\\n| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |\\r\\n| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntroduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}}'}]

The other is required by that same rule.</figcaption>\r\n  </figure>;\r\n  if (unitId === \'group-1\') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-sufficient-figure">\r\n    <p className="co-kicker">Read the complete idea</p>\r\n    <p className="co-hero-sentence">A report is reviewed <mark>if</mark> <strong>it is submitted before noon.</strong></p>\r\n    <div className="co-hero-target"><span>What is enough for review?</span><strong>Submission before noon</strong></div>\r\n    <figcaption>The sufficient condition can come last in the sentence.</figcaption>\r\n  </figure>;\r\n  return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-required-figure">\r\n    <p className="co-kicker">Read what the sentence needs</p>\r\n    <p className="co-hero-sentence">Entry requires a <strong>current pass.</strong></p>\r\n    <div className="co-pass-figure"><span>Required for entry</span><strong>A current pass</strong><small>An expired pass won\'t meet this requirement.</small></div>\r\n    <figcaption>Having the pass alone doesn\'t establish entry.</figcaption>\r\n  </figure>;\r\n}\r\n\r\nexport function ConditionalOpeningLesson({ unit, onComplete, continueHref, continueLabel = \'Continue to guided practice\', previousHref, reviewed }: ConditionalLessonNoteProps) {\r\n  const [motionPaused, setMotionPaused] = useState(false);\r\n  const [activeInteraction, setActiveInteraction] = useState<HTMLElement | null>(null);\r\n  const [tocHidden, setTocHidden] = useState(false);\r\n  useEffect(() => {\r\n    if (!activeInteraction) { setTocHidden(false); return; }\r\n    setTocHidden(true);\r\n    if (typeof IntersectionObserver === \'undefined\') return;\r\n    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => setTocHidden(entry.isIntersecting));\r\n    observer.observe(activeInteraction);\r\n    return () => observer.disconnect();\r\n  }, [activeInteraction]);\r\n  const protectInteraction = (target: EventTarget) => {\r\n    if (!(target instanceof HTMLElement)) return;\r\n    const interaction = target.closest<HTMLElement>(\'.co-lab,.co-think-pause,.co-flash,.co-check\');\r\n    if (interaction) { setTocHidden(true); setActiveInteraction(interaction); }\r\n    else if (target.closest(\'button,a\')) setActiveInteraction(null);\r\n  };\r\n  const group = unit.id === \'group-1\' || unit.id === \'group-2\';\r\n  const orientation = unit.id === \'orientation\';\r\n  const title = group ? unit.id === \'group-1\' ? \'Group 1: what is enough?\' : \'Group 2: what is required?\' : orientation ? \'What Are Conditionals?\' : \'What is enough? What is required?\';\r\n  const repairTitle = orientation ? "Don\'t turn a rule into a case fact" : unit.id === \'sufficient-necessary\' ? "Don\'t turn a requirement into a guarantee" : unit.id === \'group-1\' ? \'Keep the direction and the whole condition\' : \'Keep requirements and completed actions distinct\';\r\n  const subtitle = orientation\r\n    ? "A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too. Let\'s see what that connection lets you conclude, and what it leaves open."\r\n    : group ? unit.id === \'group-1\'\r\n      ? \'These expressions can point to the sufficient idea. Read what each one says before deciding which complete condition belongs on the left.\'\r\n      : \'These expressions can identify a requirement. Find the complete idea the sentence says you need, then check what its presence does and does not prove.\'\r\n    : "The same rule gives its two conditions different jobs. Let\'s take each one seriously before giving it a place in the diagram.";\r\n  return <div className="conditional-note co-opening" data-motion-paused={motionPaused} onFocusCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)} onClickCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)}>\r\n    <div className="co-lesson-navigation" hidden={tocHidden}><DynamicIslandTOC key={unit.id} selector=".co-opening [data-toc]"/></div>\r\n    <header className={\'co-hero \' + (orientation ? \'co-intro-hero\' : \'\')}>\r\n      <div className="co-hero-copy"><p className="co-kicker">Conditional logic</p><h1>{title}</h1><p>{subtitle}</p>\r\n      <a href="#co-reading">{orientation ? \'Start with the meaning\' : \'Begin reading\'} <span aria-hidden="true">↓</span></a></div>\r\n      <OpeningFigure unitId={unit.id}/>\r\n    </header>\r\n    <article id="co-reading">\r\n      {group ? <GroupLessons id={unit.id}/> : <Fundamentals roles={unit.id === \'sufficient-necessary\'}/>}\r\n      <ConditionalOpeningMedia unitId={unit.id}/>\r\n      <Section id="repair" title={repairTitle}>\r\n        <div className="co-word-comparison"><div><h3>The tempting move</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.mistake}</p></div><div><h3>Read the sentence again</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.repair}</p></div></div>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <footer className="co-footer">\r\n        <button type="button" className="co-motion-toggle" aria-pressed={motionPaused} onClick={() => setMotionPaused(!motionPaused)}>Reduce animation in this lesson</button>\r\n        <p>{reviewed ? \'You marked this lesson as read. You can return to the examples whenever you need.\' : \'Marking this lesson as read records review. Your practice gives you a chance to use the reasoning yourself.\'}</p>\r\n        <div className="co-choices">{previousHref && <Link to={previousHref}>Previous lesson</Link>}<button type="button" onClick={onComplete}>{reviewed ? \'Lesson read\' : \'Mark lesson as read\'}</button><Link className="co-primary" to={continueHref}>{continueLabel} <span aria-hidden="true">→</span></Link></div>\r\n      </footer>\r\n    </article>\r\n  </div>;\r\n}\r\n'}]

text((await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nfrom pathlib import Path\np=Path('src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx')\ns=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')\nstart=s.index('    {!roles && <>',s.index('function Fundamentals'))\nend=s.index('    {roles && <>',start)\nold=s[start:end]\nnuance_start=old.index('        <p>Keep the sentence\\'s own strength.')\nnuance_end=old.index('      </Section>',nuance_start)\nnuance=old[nuance_start:nuance_end]\nnew='''    {!roles && <>\n      <Section title=\"One condition tells you something else\">\n        <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something we're considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. We can ask whether each one is true.</p>\n        <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn't tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow <strong>if</strong> it did.</p>\n        <p>Think of that if as an invitation to imagine a situation. Suppose the first condition is true. What would you then know? We can ask that question without claiming the situation really happened.</p>\n        <IfConnection/>\n        <p>You don't need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Why learn this?\" dark>\n        <p>An LSAT question gives you some information and asks what you can conclude from it. Sometimes that information is a rule about what has to be true if something else is true. You need to know what the rule says before using it.</p>\n        <p>A rule can tell you something about an order, a person, or a decision. It can also leave questions unanswered. We'll practice telling the difference between what we've been told and what we're adding ourselves.</p>\n        <p>Let's begin with one rule. We'll read it, imagine a case that meets its condition, and ask what we know about that same case.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Read one rule in ordinary English\">\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\n        <p>There are two ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready for pickup</strong>, and it <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence connects them. Imagine an order with that ready label. The rule tells you it has been packed.</p>\n        <p>We haven't been told that any particular order has that label. We're saying what would be true <strong>if</strong> it did. That's the difference between a rule and a fact about an actual order.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Enough on one side, required on the other\">\n        <p>Suppose an order is marked ready for pickup. Is that enough to know it's packed? Yes. The sentence says an order with that label has been packed. We don't need a second fact about its contents.</p>\n        <p>That's a <strong>guarantee</strong>: the rule doesn't leave room for a ready order that hasn't been packed. It gives us something certain about any order that meets that condition.</p>\n        <div className=\"co-role-pair\"><div><h3>Sufficient means enough</h3><p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know the order has been packed. We call it the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>.</p></div><div><h3>Necessary means required</h3><p>An order with that ready label has to have been packed. Packing is required for it. We call it the <strong>necessary condition</strong>.</p></div></div>\n        <p>These are two jobs in one rule. One condition is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first is true. We'll give each job more attention in the next lesson.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"A guarantee is stronger than a tendency\">\n        <p>Now compare a guarantee with something that is only usually true.</p>\n        <div className=\"co-intro-relationship\"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready for pickup are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</strong><p>Every order with that label has to be packed.</p></div></div>\n        <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. A ready order that hasn't been packed could fit the first statement. It couldn't fit the second.</p>\n        <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we're asking what follows from this statement. We aren't checking whether the shop follows its rule in real life.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Use a fact about one order\">\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\n        <p>Now add a fact: <strong>Order A is marked ready for pickup.</strong> This is no longer just an imagined case. The question has told us that A meets the condition.</p>\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\"Order A is marked ready for pickup. What do we now know about it?\">\n          <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order with that ready label. The fact tells us A has it. Together, they let us conclude A has been packed.</p>\n          <p>The rule alone didn't tell us A was ready. The separate fact did that. Our conclusion is about A, not every order in the shop.</p>\n        </ThinkPause>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"The arrow is a shorter way to write it\">\n        <p>We can record that relationship with shorter labels. Here, <strong>Marked ready</strong> means marked ready for pickup, and <strong>Packed</strong> means the same order has been packed.</p>\n        <Arrow left=\"Marked ready\" right=\"Packed\"/>\n        <p>The sufficient condition goes on the left: it gives us enough to know the other condition is true. The necessary condition goes on the right: it's required whenever the left condition is true.</p>\n        <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>: if the order is marked ready for pickup, it must have been packed. It doesn't mean “and then,” and it doesn't say the label causes packing. This is a connection between what is true, not a timeline.</p>\n        <p>English doesn't always put the sufficient idea first. <strong>An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup</strong> expresses the same rule with the ideas in a different order. The ready label still gives us packing, so the diagram stays the same. Meaning decides the two sides.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Change the fact, keep the rule\">\n        <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, we're told about packing instead of the ready label.</p>\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\"Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready for pickup?\">\n          <p><strong>We don't know.</strong> B could have the ready label. It could also be packed without that label. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>\n          <p>The rule tells us about orders marked ready for pickup. It hasn't said every packed order gets that label. Packing alone doesn't answer whether B is ready.</p>\n        </ThinkPause>\n        <p>In the drawing, orders marked ready sit inside packed orders. Every ready order has to be packed. A packed order could be inside or outside the ready group. Compare the two facts to see what each one tells us.</p>\n        <SetExplorer/>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Try the same reasoning with a new rule\">\n        <p>You used the ready label to establish packing. Starting with packing left the label unknown. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>\n        <Check prompt=\"Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?\" options={['Yes, the badge proves it.', 'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.']} correct={1} feedback={['You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.', 'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.']}/>\n        <p>A badge may make volunteer status seem plausible. Check whether the sentence actually told you that everyone with a badge is a volunteer. This one didn't.</p>\n      </Section>\n      <Section title=\"Keep what the sentence actually gives you\">\n'''+nuance+'''      </Section>\n    </>}\n'''\ns=s[:start]+new+s[end:]\ns=s.replace('Every order marked ready has been packed.', 'If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.')\ns=s.replace(\"<Check prompt=\\\"The only vehicles permitted inside are electric. Which diagram preserves the rule?\\\" options={['Electric → Permitted inside', 'Permitted inside → Electric']} correct={1} feedback={['You made electric status enough for permission. The sentence requires permitted vehicles to be electric; it does not promise admission to every electric vehicle.', 'Permitted vehicles have to be electric. This direction preserves the limit on permitted vehicles without adding a promise about all electric vehicles.']}/>\",\"<Check prompt=\\\"The only notebooks kept on this shelf are hardback. Which diagram preserves the rule?\\\" options={['Hardback → Kept on shelf', 'Kept on shelf → Hardback']} correct={1} feedback={['You made being hardback enough for a place on the shelf. The sentence limits the notebooks kept there; it does not put every hardback notebook on this shelf.', 'A notebook kept on this shelf has to be hardback. A hardback notebook on another shelf would also fit the rule, so this direction keeps what the sentence actually says.']}/>\")\np.write_text(s,encoding='utf-8')\n'@ | py -","workdir":"C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-conditional-bootcamp","max_output_tokens":1000,"login":false})).output);

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '      "A conditional tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. A condition is something we can say about a situation, such as an order being marked ready or its having been packed. The conditional connects those two ideas. It doesn\'t tell us that either one has happened.",\r\n      "Why learn this? A conclusion on the LSAT can depend on which way that connection runs. Either possibility fits what we\'ve been told.",\r\n      "We can now shorten the relationship to Marked ready → Packed. Read that as: if an order is marked ready, it has been packed.
