Formatting has only been adjusted for readability and flow.\r\n4: The Art of the If/Then\r\n5: Conditional reasoning is the art of the if/then. If one thing happens, then another thing must happen. That’s it.\r\n6: Let’s explore our first conditional relationship:\r\n7: If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n8: Because you’re a thinking human, you can probably guess what this means at a literal level. If I’m breathing, there’s air around. The core of the relationship is that simple. If we have one thing, we must have the other thing. Just like with the Power Players, we have to take this MUST seriously. There is no way around this relationship. If you have the first thing, you have to have the second thing.\r\n9: For all the ballyhoo about conditional reasoning, you would think it’s a lot more complicated than that. The key to keeping conditional reasoning simple through its many applications, which we will examine in detail throughout this chapter, is understanding the concept behind it. I want you to intuitively understand the relationship at the heart of conditionals. Once you have that, the verbal idiosyncrasies that separate one conditional from another will be nothing more than blips on the radar.\r\n10: Let’s start by taking our conditional example apart piece by piece.\r\n11: The Sufficient Condition\r\n12: The “if” part of the conditional is called the sufficient condition.\r\n13: If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n14: The sufficient condition is a door opener. It’s welcoming. It could occur, but it doesn’t have to. Just like an open door, someone may walk in, but they don’t have to. In the example, I might be breathing or I might not: I could be holding my breath trying to get rid of hiccups or I could be breathing. We don’t know. We just know what will happen if I am breathing.\r\n15: If the sufficient condition is absent, you can completely ignore the conditional statement. If I’m not breathing, this conditional doesn’t matter at all. There might be air; there might not be air. The conditional wasn’t activated, so who cares? I’m not breathing, so it doesn’t matter.\r\n16: But if the sufficient condition is present, the conditional statement must be followed. If I am breathing, there absolutely 100% has to be air. The conditional statement gets activated and everything goes according to plan.\r\n17: What does it mean to activate a conditional?\r\n18: It means the sufficient condition is present! So the conditional must be followed.\r\n19: The sufficient condition extends beyond just the word “if.” There is a whole family of words that work just like “if”; they let you know you have a sufficient condition on your hands.\r\n20: Here are the members of the sufficient family:\r\n21: | Sufficient Indicators | Example\r\n22: | if | If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n23: | when(ever) | When I’m breathing, air is present.\r\n24: Whenever I’m breathing, air is present.\r\n25: | any(time) | Any breathing requires that air is present.\r\n26: Anytime I’m breathing, air is present.\r\n27: | all | All instances of breathing have air present.\r\n28: | every(time) | Every instance of breathing has air present.\r\n29: Everytime I’m breathing, air is present.\r\n30: | in order to | In order to breathe, I must have air.\r\n31: | people who | People who breathe need air.\r\n32: | each | Each instance of breathing has air.\r\n33: Notice how all of the sufficient family members have similar personalities. They are all inclusive, open words. Think back to the similarities between these words if/when you get lost in complex language.\r\n34: I had to smooth out some of the linguistic rough edges to translate that conditional into all the different versions, but the core relationship remained the same. Language is the challenge of conditionals; you have to distill the same conditional relationship from many different wording combinations. This is why it’s so important to understand the relationship on a deeper level than “look for indicator words.” The words are the hard part, so relying on indicators exclusively is a ticking time bomb.\r\n35: What’s with all the parentheses? What does when(ever) mean?\r\n36: The parentheses are referring to an optional addition to the original word. Both “when” and “whenever” are members of the sufficient family. Conditionally, all of the examples in the chart above mean exactly the same thing as our initial example.\r\n37: The Necessary Condition\r\n38: The “then” part of the conditional is called the necessary condition.\r\n39: If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n40: The necessary condition slams the door that the sufficient condition opens. It is the place of certainty. I mean just look at its name! It’s called the necessary condition because it is literally necessary. It has to occur at some point if the conditional relationship is activated. You can prove things that live in the necessary condition.\r\n41: There is a family of words that serves the same purpose as “then.” Each of these words lets you know you have a necessary condition in play. Here are the members of the necessary family:\r\n42: | Necessary Indicators | Example\r\n43: | then | If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n44: | must | Air must be present for breathing.\r\n45: | necessary | Air is necessary for breathing.\r\n46: | required | Air is required for breathing.\r\n47: | only (if) | Only the presence of air allows for breathing.\r\n48: I’m breathing, only if air is present.\r\n49: | depends | Breathing depends on air being present.\r\n50: | need (to) | I need air to breathe.\r\n51: | have to | I have to have air, if I’m breathing.\r\n52: | essential | Air is essential for breathing.\r\n53: | precondition | Air is a precondition for breathing.\r\n54: Notice the similarities in each of these words. They are all ironclad words. They are serious and restrictive; they are obligations.\r\n55: All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.\r\n56: Pay special attention to ONLY IF! It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.\r\n57: The Contrapositive\r\n58: So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:\r\n59: If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.\r\n60: This relationship is ironclad. We’ll call it the Must Relationship. Remember this as we explore a conditional thought experiment.\r\n61: Imagine you’re a coffee-obsessed individual. If you’re walking, you’re on your way to get a coffee. Getting a coffee is the only reason you ever walk anywhere. Every single time you walk, you are getting a coffee.\r\n62: Now, imagine that you are not on your way to get a coffee.\r\n63: What do you know about yourself?\r\n64: Put your guess here: ______________________________\r\n65: STOP\r\n66: Really try to figure this out right now. Don’t read the solution until you have a guess. I put this question to every student. The ones that hazard a guess (either voluntarily or under protest) always come away better off.\r\n67: If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.\r\n68: The fact that you are not on your way to get a coffee means you are not walking.\r\n69: REMEMBER!\r\n70: If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.\r\n71: As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized. If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.\r\n72: Let’s return to our original example to see how its contrapositive looks:\r\n73: If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n74: OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.\r\n75: The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship. The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.\r\n76: Check out this chart showing what does and does not activate your conditional relationship:\r\n77: | Situations | What Has to Happen? | If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.\r\n78: | Sufficient Present | Must have necessary condition | I’m breathing, so there must be air.\r\n79: | Necessary Absent | Can’t have sufficient condition | There’s no air, so I can’t breathe.\r\n80: | Sufficient Absent | Nothing! | There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.\r\n81: | Necessary Present | Nothing! | There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.\r\n82: The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.\r\n83: How to Diagram Conditionals\r\n84: Now that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.\r\n85: | IF — Sufficient Condition | → | THEN — Necessary Condition\r\n86: |\r\n87: when(ever)\r\n88: any(time)\r\n89: all\r\n90: every(time)\r\n91: in order to\r\n92: people who\r\n93: each\r\n94: | →\r\n95: |\r\n96: must\r\n97: necessary\r\n98: required\r\n99: only (if)\r\n100: depends\r\n101: need (to)\r\n102: have to\r\n103: essential\r\n104: precondition\r\n105: The Must Arrow\r\n106: The conditional relationship is ideal for symbolic representation because it is always the same underneath the varying language. We symbolize the conditional relationship with one simple sign:\r\n107: Sufficient → Necessary\r\n108: ↑\r\n109: Must Arrow\r\n110: We use the Must Arrow to signify the Must Relationship. It is a reminder of the urgency of conditionals. Every conditional relationship can be written like this. We call it diagramming the conditional.\r\n111: Here are your steps for diagramming a conditional relationship:\r\n112: See a conditional indicator.\r\n113: Draw a Must Arrow.\r\n114: Place an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.\r\n115: Sufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.\r\n116: Necessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.\r\n117: Fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with whatever is left over in the statement.\r\n118: Let’s take diagramming conditionals for a spin together. We’ll start with our favorite statement:\r\n119: If I’m breathing, then air is present.\r\n120: I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a B in the sufficient condition:\r\n121: B →\r\n122: Now, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with A for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.\r\n123: B → A\r\n124: There you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:\r\n125: ~A → ~B\r\n126: Check out the little squiggle (~) in front of the ~A and ~B. We’ll use the squiggle throughout this text to negate variables. Read the squiggle as a “not.”\r\n127: Check out a few more contrapositive examples:\r\n128: | Original Statement | Equivalent Contrapositive\r\n129: | A → B | ~B → ~A\r\n130: | Q → ~R | R → ~Q\r\n131: | ~L → P | ~P → L\r\n132: | ~Y → ~X | X → Y\r\n133: Once you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.\r\n134: I don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive. Both statements are describing the same relationship.\r\n135: Instead of writing the contrapositive out every time, you can just read the contrapositive when it’s relevant. Every conditional can be read two ways:\r\n136: Read it forwards from left to right.\r\n137: Read it backwards from right to left, negating everything as you go (reading the contrapositive without having to write it out).\r\n138: Awesome! Let’s talk through some frequent diagramming issues together.\r\n139: Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ\r\n140: Q.\r\n141: When should I diagram a conditional?\r\n142: A.\r\n143: In the beginning, you should always diagram conditionals. This trains you to make sure you’re actually thinking conditionally. The language in LR is your biggest obstacle. Diagramming the conditional gets rid of all the linguistic ambiguity. You don’t have to look back at the text once you’re sure you diagrammed the conditional correctly. (I’ll get to how you’ll be sure in a second. Hold tight.)\r\n144: When you are more experienced, you should diagram whenever the stimulus is not easy. I always err on the side of diagramming; I think of it as using the paper to my advantage. If I can take down a note to free myself of the exact words of the stimulus, I’m going to do it. I see far more problems from students declining to diagram than I do from students who diagram too much. If there is more than one conditional in a stimulus, you should diagram them to see if there is chaining potential.\r\n145: Besides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.\r\n146: Q.\r\n147: How will I ever be sure my conditional is correctly diagrammed?\r\n148: A.\r\n149: Seeing it work out correctly enough times breeds confidence. In your Wrong Answer Journal, keep track of every time you incorrectly diagram a conditional and make note of exactly how the language tricked you. Study these errors so you don’t make the same mistake again. Your mistakes are your greatest asset.\r\n150: The only way to ever be 100% sure that your diagram is right is confidence in your own abilities. There will never be a little green checkmark that spontaneously appears on the page to tell you that you’re right.\r\n151: Everyone messes this up in the beginning. You can’t let that kill your confidence. Do the work, build skill, and you will eventually know that you’re right.\r\n152: Q.\r\n153: But diagramming takes sooooo much time. I need to be fast. Do I really have to?\r\n154: A.\r\n155: Diagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.\r\n156: Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.\r\n157: Q.\r\n158: Should I always diagram the contrapositive?\r\n159: A.\r\n160: No. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.\r\n161: That said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.\r\n162: You’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.\r\n163: Q.\r\n164: What’s this abbreviation stuff?\r\n165: A.\r\n166: When you diagram, you should shorten each condition to a capital letter or two. For example:\r\n167: If I’m a chair, then I’m furniture.\r\n168: We would shorten the “chair” to C and shorten the “furniture” to F. Ending up with this diagram:\r\n169: C → F\r\n170: For more complex conditions, just use the minimum number of letters that you will actually understand. Say one of the conditions is “maximum return on investment,” you should shorten that to MRI or MR… Whichever option calls up the condition in your mind will work. Don’t write out the whole word or more than four letters per condition. The notation becomes cumbersome when you write more than that.\r\n171: Figuring Out The Language\r\n172: Let’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.\r\n173: The indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.\r\n174: In this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.\r\n175: | If [I’m breathing], then air is present. | Each [instance of breathing] has air.\r\n176: | When [I’m breathing], air is present. | I’m breathing, only if [air is present].\r\n177: | Any [breathing] means air is present. | Only [the presence of air] allows for breathing.\r\n178: | All [instances of breathing] have air present. | Breathing depends on [air being present].\r\n179: | Every [instance of breathing] has air present. | I need [air] to breathe.\r\n180: | In order to [breathe], I must have air. | I have to have [air], if I’m breathing.\r\n181: | People who [breathe] need air. |\r\n182: Notice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:\r\n183: | [Air] must be present for breathing. | [Air] is essential for breathing.\r\n184: | [Air] is necessary for breathing. | [Air] is a precondition for breathing.\r\n185: | [Air] is required for breathing. |\r\n186: In these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is really saying. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:\r\n187: [Air] must be present for breathing. / There must be [air] for us to breathe.\r\n188: [Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].\r\n189: [Air] is required for breathing. / I require [air] to breathe.\r\n190: [Final line at the bottom of the image is partially obscured.]\r\n191: Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then\r\n192: Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.\r\n193: Here’s another safeguard on conditional diagramming. Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens.\r\n194: Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation. This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.\r\n195: How to Create a Personalized If/Then\r\n196: Imagine you’re confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking:\r\n197: Try to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning of the sentence.\r\n198: Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.\r\n199: Put yourself in your guess’ situation and ask what you know about yourself.\r\n200: If your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.\r\n201: Let’s translate an example into a personalized if/then:\r\n202: Orchids depend on frequent conversation.\r\n203: Before you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:\r\n204: The key to intuitively understanding conditionals is personalizing them. Always put yourself in the shoes of the conditional, no matter what it’s about.\r\n205: So, if I’m an orchid, what do I know about me? I know that I have to have frequent conversation. I depend on frequent conversation. If I’m an orchid, then I have frequent conversation. This fits. It is the correct translation of the conditional.\r\n206: Now, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:\r\n207: So, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t need anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.\r\n208: The personalized if/then is how I always do conditionals, unless they are already phrased as if/thens. It gives me confidence throughout the test that I’m not making a mistake.\r\n209: Basic Conditional Drill — Much Ado About Nothing\r\n210: The conditional drills in this chapter are each inspired by Shakespeare plays, which you will see identified in the title of each drill. The lines are very lightly edited to highlight their conditionality, but these are more-or-less the real lines from Shakespeare. And yes, Shakespeare is really this outlandish; high school didn’t do him justice.\r\n211: Instructions\r\n212: Diagram the following conditional statements. Don’t be afraid to look back at the section, if you need any help.\r\n213: If he were in my books, I’d burn my study.\r\n214: Original: B → S\r\n215: Contrapositive: ~S → ~B\r\n216: Every time I hear my dog bark at a crow, it’s like hearing a man swear he loves me.\r\n217: Original: DBC → CM\r\n218: Contrapositive: ~CM → ~DBC\r\n219: If I were a man, I would eat his heart in the marketplace.\r\n220: Original: IM → EHM\r\n221: Contrapositive: ~EHM → ~IM\r\n222: I am heart-burned an hour after whenever I see him.\r\n223: Original: SH → HB\r\n224: Contrapositive: ~HB → ~SH\r\n225: Benedick is always a rare parrot-teacher.\r\n226: Original: B → RPT\r\n227: Contrapositive: ~RPT → ~B\r\n228: Love requires me to transform into an oyster.\r\n229: Original: L → TO\r\n230: Contrapositive: ~TO → ~L\r\n231: Where’s the answer key?\r\n232: You’ll find the answer key at the end of the chapter on page 148.\r\n'}]

\'no-link\']\r\nand-or | Keep AND and OR inside the right scope | prereqs: [\'triggering\', \'negation\']\r\nde-morgan | Negate compound statements accurately | prereqs: [\'and-or\', \'contraposition\']\r\ndomain | Keep rules inside their stated domain | prereqs: [\'implicit-conditionals\', \'triggering\', \'and-or\']\r\nbiconditionals | Recognize rules that run both ways | prereqs: [\'mixed-translation\', \'contraposition\', \'and-or\']\r\nwhole-rule-negation | Deny the rule with a counterexample | prereqs: [\'domain\', \'de-morgan\', \'biconditionals\']\r\nmbt | Draw only conclusions the facts force | prereqs: [\'triggering\', \'de-morgan\', \'domain\']\r\nsufficient-assumption | Build a bridge that guarantees the conclusion | prereqs: [\'mbt\', \'direct-linking\', \'no-link\', \'and-or\']\r\nnecessary-assumption | Find what the argument cannot do without | prereqs: [\'sufficient-assumption\', \'whole-rule-negation\']\r\nflaw | Explain the invalid move | prereqs: [\'mbt\', \'whole-rule-negation\']\r\nparallel | Match the argument’s structure | prereqs: [\'flaw\', \'de-morgan\', \'domain\']\r\nprinciple | Apply a rule without overstating a principle | prereqs: [\'sufficient-assumption\', \'mbt\', \'domain\']\r\nquantifier-meaning | Read some, most and all precisely | prereqs: [\'whole-rule-negation\', \'mbt\']\r\nquantifier-negation | Deny quantities without overshooting | prereqs: [\'quantifier-meaning\', \'whole-rule-negation\']\r\nquantifier-linking | Carry witnesses and majorities through valid links | prereqs: [\'quantifier-meaning\', \'quantifier-negation\', \'direct-linking\', \'domain\']\r\nquantifier-invalid | Recognize where quantity chains stop | prereqs: [\'quantifier-linking\', \'flaw\']\r\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '        "Here must states what finalists are required to do. If someone is a finalist, that person has an obligation to attend the interview.",\r\n        "The sentence hasn\'t supplied a fact proving that every finalist complied. Keep required to in the complete target so that your diagram preserves the difference between an obligation and a completed action.",\r\n      ],\r\n      prompt: \'Maya is a finalist. Which follows: she is required to attend, or she actually attended?\',\r\n      reasoning: [\r\n        "She is required to attend. Maya is a finalist, and the sentence gives every finalist that obligation.",\r\n        "Actual attendance would need information about what Maya did. Finalist → Attends interview would quietly turn the stated requirement into proof of compliance.",\r\n      ],\r\n      after: [\r\n        "Shortening a label should save writing while preserving the claim. Required to attend interview is longer than attends interview, but here those extra words are doing necessary work.",\r\n      ],\r\n    },\r\n  ],\r\n};\r\n\r\nfunction IndicatorSections({ id }: { id: string }) {\r\n  return <>{indicatorExplanations[id].map((lesson, index) => {\r\n    const item = translationExamples[id][index];\r\n    return <Section key={item.word} title={lesson.title} expressionNumber={index + 1}>\r\n      <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>\r\n      {lesson.paragraphs.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\r\n      {id === \'group-2\' && item.word === \'only if\' && <>\r\n        <p>Try reading the sentence as though it concerns you. Imagine that you graduate and ask what you now have to know about your project. Then try starting from project completion and see whether the sentence settles graduation.</p>\r\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Put yourself in the proposed starting condition, then check what the original sentence requires you to know. We\'re checking its meaning, not predicting what usually happens to graduates.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="If I graduate, what must be true of my project?">\r\n          <p>I\'ve completed it. The sentence requires project completion for anyone who graduates, including me in this imagined case.</p>\r\n          <p>Graduates → Completes project matches that reading. Now try the reverse below.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n      </>}\r\n      <ThinkPause prompt={lesson.prompt}>\r\n        {lesson.reasoning.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\r\n        <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>\r\n        <p className="co-readback">{item.readback}</p>\r\n      </ThinkPause>\r\n      {lesson.after?.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\r\n      {lesson.check && <Check {...lesson.check}/>}\r\n      {id === \'group-2\' && item.word === \'only if\' && <DirectionSwitch/>}\r\n    </Section>;\r\n  })}</>;\r\n}\r\n\r\nfunction Fundamentals({ roles = false }: { roles?: boolean }) {\r\n  return <>\r\n    {!roles && <>\r\n      <Section title="One condition tells you something else">\r\n        <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something we\'re considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. We can ask whether each one is true.</p>\r\n        <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn\'t tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow <strong className="co-if-inline">if</strong> it did.</p>\r\n        <p>Think of that if as an invitation to imagine a situation. Suppose the first condition is true. What would you then know? We can ask that question without claiming the situation really happened.</p>\r\n        <IfConnection/>\r\n        <p>You don\'t need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Why learn this?" dark>\r\n        <p>An LSAT question gives you some information and asks what you can conclude from it. Sometimes that information is a rule about what has to be true if something else is true. You need to know what the rule says before using it.</p>\r\n        <p>A rule can tell you something about an order, a person, or a decision. It can also leave questions unanswered. We\'ll practice telling the difference between what we\'ve been told and what we\'re adding ourselves.</p>\r\n        <p>Let\'s begin with one rule. We\'ll read it, imagine a case that meets its condition, and ask what we know about that same case.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Read one rule in ordinary English">\r\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>There are two ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready for pickup</strong>, and it <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence connects them. Imagine an order with that ready label. The rule tells you it has been packed.</p>\r\n        <p>We haven\'t been told that any particular order has that label. We\'re saying what would be true <strong>if</strong> it did. That\'s the difference between a rule and a fact about an actual order.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Enough on one side, required on the other">\r\n        <p>Suppose an order is marked ready for pickup. Is that enough to know it\'s packed? Yes. The sentence says an order with that label has been packed. We don\'t need a second fact about its contents.</p>\r\n        <p>That\'s a <strong>guarantee</strong>: the rule doesn\'t leave room for a ready order that hasn\'t been packed. It gives us something certain about any order that meets that condition.</p>\r\n        <div className="co-role-pair"><div><h3>Sufficient means enough</h3><p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know the order has been packed. We call it the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>.</p></div><div><h3>Necessary means required</h3><p>An order with that ready label has to have been packed. Packing is required for it. We call it the <strong>necessary condition</strong>.</p></div></div>\r\n        <p>These are two jobs in one rule. One condition is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first is true. We\'ll give each job more attention in the next lesson.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="A guarantee is stronger than a tendency">\r\n        <p>Now compare a guarantee with something that is only usually true.</p>\r\n        <div className="co-intro-relationship"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready for pickup are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</strong><p>Every order with that label has to be packed.</p></div></div>\r\n        <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. A ready order that hasn\'t been packed could fit the first statement. It couldn\'t fit the second.</p>\r\n        <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we\'re asking what follows from this statement. We aren\'t checking whether the shop follows its rule in real life.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Use a fact about one order">\r\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>Now add a fact: <strong>Order A is marked ready for pickup.</strong> This is no longer just an imagined case. The question has told us that A meets the condition.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Order A is marked ready for pickup. What do we now know about it?">\r\n          <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order with that ready label. The fact tells us A has it. Together, they let us conclude A has been packed.</p>\r\n          <p>The rule alone didn\'t tell us A was ready. The separate fact did that. Our conclusion is about A, not every order in the shop.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="The arrow is a shorter way to write it">\r\n        <p>We can record that relationship with shorter labels. Here, <strong>Marked ready</strong> means marked ready for pickup, and <strong>Packed</strong> means the same order has been packed.</p>\r\n        <Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/>\r\n        <p>The sufficient condition goes on the left: it gives us enough to know the other condition is true. The necessary condition goes on the right: it\'s required whenever the left condition is true.</p>\r\n        <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>: if the order is marked ready for pickup, it must have been packed. It doesn\'t mean “and then,” and it doesn\'t say the label causes packing. This is a connection between what is true, not a timeline.</p>\r\n        <p>English doesn\'t always put the sufficient idea first. <strong>An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup</strong> expresses the same rule with the ideas in a different order. The ready label still gives us packing, so the diagram stays the same. Meaning decides the two sides.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Change the fact, keep the rule">\r\n        <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, we\'re told about packing instead of the ready label.</p>\r\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready for pickup?">\r\n          <p><strong>We don\'t know.</strong> B could have the ready label. It could also be packed without that label. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>\r\n          <p>The rule tells us about orders marked ready for pickup. It hasn\'t said every packed order gets that label. Packing alone doesn\'t answer whether B is ready.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n        <p>In the drawing, orders marked ready sit inside packed orders. Every ready order has to be packed. A packed order could be inside or outside the ready group. Compare the two facts to see what each one tells us.</p>\r\n        <SetExplorer/>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Try the same reasoning with a new rule">\r\n        <p>You used the ready label to establish packing. Starting with packing left the label unknown. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>\r\n        <Check prompt="Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?" options={[\'Yes, the badge proves it.\', \'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.\', \'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.\']}/>\r\n        <p>A badge may make volunteer status seem plausible. Check whether the sentence actually told you that everyone with a badge is a volunteer. This one didn\'t.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Keep what the sentence actually gives you">\r\n        <p>Keep the sentence\'s own strength. A rule can give you permission to do something without saying you did it. It can also tell you what you\'re supposed to do without reporting that you followed the instruction. Those are different claims.</p>\r\n        <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>May use gives permission here. A guest with a ticket is allowed to use the cloakroom. That guest could leave their coat on and never go inside. The permission is still there.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?">\r\n          <p>No. We know she\'s allowed to use it because the rule gives that permission to every guest with a ticket. We haven\'t been told whether she used it.</p>\r\n          <p>Compare this instruction: <strong>If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack.</strong> A wet coat makes the instruction call for hanging it up. That doesn\'t report anyone carrying out the instruction. We\'d need a fact about what they actually did.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n        <Check prompt="The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\r\n          "You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn\'t reported that anyone did it.",\r\n          "The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.",\r\n          "The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn\'t say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.",\r\n        ]}/>\r\n        <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn\'t used, and told to return isn\'t returned.</p>\r\n      </Section>\r\n    </>}\r\n    {roles && <>\r\n      <Section title="Sufficient means enough">\r\n        <p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know an order has been packed. That makes the ready label <strong>sufficient</strong> for packing. Sufficient means enough.</p>\r\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>The rule already tells you what has to be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?">\r\n          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>\r\n          <p>This doesn\'t make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Enough does not mean the only way">\r\n        <p>Suppose Order D isn\'t marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn\'t settle the packing question.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Order D isn\'t marked ready. Could it still have been packed?">\r\n          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn\'t been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>\r\n          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn\'t said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn\'t present, the other condition may still be true.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\r\n        <Check prompt="Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?" options={[\'Marked collected\', \'Has a pickup receipt\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.\', \'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.\']}/>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Necessary means required">\r\n        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready for pickup has to have been packed. Packing is required for an order with that label. That makes being packed <strong>necessary</strong> for being marked ready.</p>\r\n        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>\r\n        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Which condition does a ready order have to meet?">\r\n          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we\'d have a ready order that wasn\'t packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>\r\n          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n        <div className="co-role-pair"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\r\n        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>\r\n        <GateExplorer/>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Having a requirement does not guarantee the result">\r\n        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\r\n        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\r\n        <Arrow left="Enters the archive" right="Has a pass"/>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?">\r\n          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\r\n          <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\r\n        </ThinkPause>\r\n        <Check prompt="Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', "You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too."]}/>\r\n      </Section>\r\n      <Section title="Try the sentence from the inside">\r\n        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\r\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\r\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\r\n        <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>\r\n        <ThinkPause prompt="If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?">\r\n          <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4d9b7a","wall_time_seconds":0.2114201,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4710,"output":"# Conditional opening: the reference now sets the sequence\\r\\n\\r\\n## Current skeleton selected on 2026-10-03\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s latest instruction makes the supplied Conditional Reasoning HTML the **content-order backbone**, not just a voice reference. Every supplied conceptual beat must have a home in the same relative order. Original wording, original examples, deeper explanations and well-placed interactions are encouraged. This supersedes the earlier opening-only scope and sequence recorded below. It does not change historical assignments or mean the app already renders this order.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe OneDrive file `C:/Users/Joshu/OneDrive/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html` was read in full and matches the [archived source](../../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html) byte for byte: SHA-256 `13afbb1ecd2d4b0f80ca918d1956690e16493b1addbf273314c9c1124ad380cb`. [Voice and source limits](../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) still apply. No new source generation is needed.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source starts with the if/then relationship; it does not contain a separate condition-definition chapter. Joshua\'s requested brief definition of a condition and plain LSAT purpose belong at the start of that opening. They should prepare the source\'s first idea, not create a long detour.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The complete teaching timeline\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are authoring sections, not difficulty levels, assignment labels, or a student-facing catalogue. Each can contain several short notes and interactions. Joshua assigns the appropriate lesson or practice; a student sees that assigned work.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Order | What we teach, in sequence | What the learner does here |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **1. What a conditional tells you** | Define a condition as an idea that can be true or false. Define a conditional through if/then. Explain what the relationship lets us know and why that matters for LSAT conclusions. Read one original rule naturally. Explain that must rules out an exception to the stated relationship. Distinguish imagining the if-condition from reporting that it occurred. | Read the rule; describe what would follow **if** its first condition were true. A brief meaning check comes before notation. |\\r\\n| **2. Sufficient: enough to establish the other condition** | Name the if-part. Explain that it may or may not be true. If it is absent, this rule alone does not settle the other condition. If it is present, the required condition follows. Define using or activating the rule through that present sufficient condition. | Compare the same rule with a sufficient-present fact and a sufficient-absent fact. Explain both conclusions before revealing the answers. |\\r\\n| **3. The sufficient word family** | Introduce the family in the source\'s row order: if; when/whenever; any/anytime; all; every/every time; in order to; people who; each. Show equivalent ways to express one relationship. Explain grouped forms and optional endings. Explain why understanding the sentence matters more than spotting a word. | Compare ordinary-English versions of a stable relationship; identify the complete sufficient idea. No arrow-construction task yet. Add current Group 1 expressions as clearly contextual extensions here. |\\r\\n| **4. Necessary: what has to be true** | Name the then-part. Explain its required role when the sufficient condition is true. Establish certainty about that requirement. Avoid implying that the condition must happen later in time. | In the same anchor, explain what is required and why. Keep the sufficient and necessary roles visible as complete ideas. |\\r\\n| **5. The necessary word family** | Follow the source\'s row order: then; must; necessary; required; only/only if; depends; need/need to; have to; essential; precondition. Show how these express the same relationship as the earlier family. Give only if special attention: it identifies a requirement despite containing if. Explain that symbols will help keep the relationship clear when wording changes. | Read and compare necessary-language sentences, including a controlled if versus only-if contrast. Add current Group 2 forms here without silently reclassifying Joshua\'s groups. |\\r\\n| **6. Discover the contrapositive** | Restate the sufficient-to-necessary relationship. Use a fresh thought experiment. Remove the necessary condition and ask whether the sufficient condition could still be true. Pause. Derive the answer from the unmet requirement. Name the contrapositive only after that reasoning, then return to the anchor. | Predict before reveal, explain the contradiction in ordinary English, and apply the reasoning to the anchor. Do not start with flip-and-negate. |\\r\\n| **7. Compare all four situations** | Teach in the source\'s chart order: sufficient present; necessary absent; sufficient absent; necessary present. The first establishes the necessary condition; the second rules out the sufficient condition; the other two leave the other condition unsettled by this rule alone. Explain the tempting reverse and inverse at this point. | Use a four-case interaction. State the strongest justified conclusion, then show an allowed case where an unsupported guess fails. Keep the rule available for later facts. |\\r\\n| **8. Write the relationship with an arrow** | Recover both word families. Introduce sufficient on the left, necessary on the right and the must-arrow between them. Explain implication, not chronology or causation. Build a first diagram incrementally: indicator, complete target, role, remaining condition. Explain each abbreviation. | Watch a narrated construction, then place complete ideas and read the result back in English. Sentence order can differ from diagram order. |\\r\\n| **9. Write and read the contrapositive** | Diagram the meaning already learned. Introduce the not-sign. Work through all four sign patterns: neither side negative, necessary negative, sufficient negative, both negative. Derive reverse-and-negate as a shortcut. Explain equivalence. Then distinguish writing the contrapositive from reading it when useful. | Build and read each form; explain why the transformed rule says the same thing. Teach Joshua\'s full five-step routine here: indicator; two complete ideas; role placement; contrapositive; English readback. |\\r\\n| **10. Answer practical diagramming questions** | Keep the source\'s FAQ order: when to diagram; how to check correctness and build confidence; concern about time; when to write a contrapositive; why training still asks for written contrapositives; how to choose readable abbreviations. | Compare a diagram with the original sentence. Diagnose a specific mistranslation and record what caused it. Start untimed; timing can be added to teacher-assigned medium/advanced practice. |\\r\\n| **11. Find the indicator\'s target** | Define target. Mark complete targets in familiar sentences. First show the usual position after an indicator. Then show targets before an indicator. Then show the same indicator moving relative to its target. The source\'s obscured line remains missing evidence, not a sentence to reconstruct. | Highlight the complete idea, including qualifications; compare meaning-preserving word-order variants. The closest noun or next word is not an automatic target rule. |\\r\\n| **12. Check with Personalized If/Then** | Define the translation check and why it helps. Read ordinary meaning; propose the sufficient condition; imagine a case meeting it; ask what must be true. Work a correct interpretation, then a deliberately reversed interpretation and explain why the latter fails. Finish by recommending the meaning check. | Perform both readings against the same whole sentence. Keep who or what the sentence concerns intact. Extend with Joshua\'s What Test as a complementary meaning check. |\\r\\n| **13. Begin with straightforward sufficient language, then varied sentence placement and necessary language. The source\'s six items model a basic translation set, not complete family coverage. | Attempt before answers. Use fresh original items and explain the target, roles, original, contrapositive and English readback. Provide both targeted family practice and a mixed transfer set. |\\r\\n\\r\\n### An original anchor for the rebuild\\r\\n\\r\\n**If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.**\\r\\n\\r\\n- Opening: imagine an order with the ready label. Packing follows under the stated rule; the sentence hasn\'t said an actual order has the label.\\r\\n- Sufficient: a ready label is enough to establish packing. Without the label, packing remains possible.\\r\\n- Necessary: packing is required for an order carrying that label.\\r\\n- Contrapositive: an order that hasn\'t been packed cannot be marked ready under this rule. Let the student work this out before naming it.\\r\\n- Four situations: ready implies packed; not packed implies not ready; not ready leaves packing open; packed leaves the ready label open.\\r\\n- Notation, introduced later: Ready → Packed; Not packed → Not ready. Abbreviate only after defining the complete labels.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis anchor is our own course example. Reuse it when a new view deepens the idea, and use fresh situations for transfer. A statement such as a published report requiring editor approval can provide a separate contrapositive prediction: no editor approval means it cannot be published under the rule. Do not treat an obligation or permission as proof that someone complied or acted.\\r\\n\\r\\n### How the current material fits\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep useful material, relocate it to the concept it trains, and add missing depth. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"215e31","wall_time_seconds":0.2568767,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3813,"output":"How to Diagram Conditionals\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nNow that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nIF — Sufficient Condition→THEN — Necessary Condition\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n    \\r\\n\\r\\n      \\r\\n        when(ever)\\r\\n        any(time)\\r\\n        all\\r\\n        every(time)\\r\\n        in order to\\r\\n        people who\\r\\n        each\\r\\n      \\r\\n      →\\r\\n      \\r\\n        must\\r\\n        necessary\\r\\n        required\\r\\n        only (if)\\r\\n        depends\\r\\n        need (to)\\r\\n        have to\\r\\n        essential\\r\\n        precondition\\r\\n      \\r\\n    \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Must Arrow\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conditional relationship is ideal for symbolic representation because it is always the same underneath the varying language. We symbolize the conditional relationship with one simple sign:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nSufficient  →  Necessary\\r\\n              ↑\\r\\n          Must Arrow\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nWe use the Must Arrow to signify the Must Relationship. It is a reminder of the urgency of conditionals. Every conditional relationship can be written like this. We call it diagramming the conditional.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere are your steps for diagramming a conditional relationship:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nSee a conditional indicator.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nDraw a Must Arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nPlace an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.\\r\\n    \\r\\n      \\r\\nSufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n      \\r\\nNecessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nFill in the other side of the Must Arrow with whatever is left over in the statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nLet’s take diagramming conditionals for a spin together. We’ll start with our favorite statement:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nIf I’m breathing, then air is present.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nI see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a B in the sufficient condition:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nB →\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nNow, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with A for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nB → A\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThere you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n~A → ~B\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck out the little squiggle (~) in front of the ~A and ~B. We’ll use the squiggle throughout this text to negate variables. Read the squiggle as a “not.”\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck out a few more contrapositive examples:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nOriginal StatementEquivalent Contrapositive\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n    \\r\\nA → B~B → ~A\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nQ → ~RR → ~Q\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\n~L → P~P → L\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\n~Y → ~XX → Y\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nOnce you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nI don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive. Both statements are describing the same relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nInstead of writing the contrapositive out every time, you can just read the contrapositive when it’s relevant. Every conditional can be read two ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n    \\r\\nRead it forwards from left to right.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nRead it backwards from right to left, negating everything as you go (reading the contrapositive without having to write it out).\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nAwesome! Let’s talk through some frequent diagramming issues together.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nQuick Conditional Diagramming FAQ\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  Q.When should I diagram a conditional?\\r\\n  A.\\r\\n    \\r\\nIn the beginning, you should always diagram conditionals. This trains you to make sure you’re actually thinking conditionally. The language in LR is your biggest obstacle. Diagramming the conditional gets rid of all the linguistic ambiguity. You don’t have to look back at the text once you’re sure you diagrammed the conditional correctly. (I’ll get to how you’ll be sure in a second. Hold tight.)\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nWhen you are more experienced, you should diagram whenever the stimulus is not easy. I always err on the side of diagramming; I think of it as using the paper to my advantage. If I can take down a note to free myself of the exact words of the stimulus, I’m going to do it. I see far more problems from students declining to diagram than I do from students who diagram too much. If there is more than one conditional in a stimulus, you should diagram them to see if there is chaining potential.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nBesides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  Q.How will I ever be sure my conditional is correctly diagrammed?\\r\\n  A.\\r\\n    \\r\\nSeeing it work out correctly enough times breeds confidence. In your Wrong Answer Journal, keep track of every time you incorrectly diagram a conditional and make note of exactly how the language tricked you. Study these errors so you don’t make the same mistake again. Your mistakes are your greatest asset.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nThe only way to ever be 100% sure that your diagram is right is confidence in your own abilities. There will never be a little green checkmark that spontaneously appears on the page to tell you that you’re right.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nEveryone messes this up in the beginning. You can’t let that kill your confidence. Do the work, build skill, and you will eventually know that you’re right.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  Q.But diagramming takes sooooo much time. I need to be fast. Do I really have to?\\r\\n  A.\\r\\n    \\r\\nDiagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nAlso, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  Q.Should I always diagram the contrapositive?\\r\\n  A.\\r\\n    \\r\\nNo. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nThat said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nYou’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  Q.What’s this abbreviation stuff?\\r\\n  A.\\r\\n    \\r\\nWhen you diagram, you should shorten each condition to a capital letter or two. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nIf I’m a chair, then I’m furniture.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nWe would shorten the “chair” to C and shorten the “furniture” to F. Ending up with this diagram:\\r\\n\\r\\n    C → F\\r\\n    \\r\\nFor more complex conditions, just use the minimum number of letters that you will actually understand. Say one of the conditions is “maximum return on investment,” you should shorten that to MRI or MR… Whichever option calls up the condition in your mind will work. Don’t write out the whole word or more than four letters per condition. The notation becomes cumbersome when you write more than that.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFiguring Out The Language\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nLet’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nIn this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n    \\r\\nIf [I’m breathing], then air is present.Each [instance of breathing] has air.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nWhen [I’m breathing], air is present.I’m breathing, only if [air is present].\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nAny [breathing] means air is present.Only [the presence of air] allows for breathing.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nAll [instances of breathing] have air present.Breathing depends on [air being present].\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nEvery [instance of breathing] has air present.I need [air] to breathe.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nIn order to [breathe], I must have air.I have to have [air], if I’m breathing.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nPeople who [breathe] need air.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n    \\r\\n[Air] must be present for breathing.[Air] is essential for breathing.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\n[Air] is necessary for breathing.[Air] is a precondition for breathing.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\n[Air] is required for breathing.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nIn these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is really saying. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n[Air] must be present for breathing. / There must be [air] for us to breathe.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n[Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n[Air] is required for breathing. / I require [air] to breathe.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n[Final line at the bottom of the image is partially obscured.]\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nYour Friend, the Personalized If/Then\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nPersonalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere’s another safeguard on conditional diagramming. Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nPersonalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation. This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHow to Create a Personalized If/Then\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nImagine you’re confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nTry to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning of the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nTake a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nPut yourself in your guess’ situation and ask what you know about yourself.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\nIf your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nLet’s translate an example into a personalized if/then:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nOrchids depend on frequent conversation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe key to intuitively understanding conditionals is personalizing them. Always put yourself in the shoes of the conditional, no matter what it’s about.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, if I’m an orchid, what do I know about me? I know that I have to have frequent conversation. I depend on frequent conversation. If I’m an orchid, then I have frequent conversation. This fits. It is the correct translation of the conditional.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nNow, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nSo, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t need anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe personalized if/then is how I always do conditionals, unless they are already phrased as if/thens. It gives me confidence throughout the test that I’m not making a mistake.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nBasic Conditional Drill — Much Ado About Nothing\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conditional drills in this chapter are each inspired by Shakespeare plays, which you will see identified in the title of each drill. The lines are very lightly edited to highlight their conditionality, but these are more-or-less the real lines from Shakespeare. And yes, Shakespeare is really this outlandish; high school didn’t do him justice.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nInstructions\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nDiagram the following conditional statements. Don’t be afraid to look back at the section, if you need any help.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nIf he were in my books, I’d burn my study.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: B → S\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~S → ~B\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nEvery time I hear my dog bark at a crow, it’s like hearing a man swear he loves me.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: DBC → CM\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~CM → ~DBC\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nIf I were a man, I would eat his heart in the marketplace.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: IM → EHM\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~EHM → ~IM\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nI am heart-burned an hour after whenever I see him.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: SH → HB\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~HB → ~SH\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nBenedick is always a rare parrot-teacher.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: B → RPT\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~RPT → ~B\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nLove requires me to transform into an oyster.\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nOriginal: L → TO\\r\\n\\r\\n    \\r\\nContrapositive: ~TO → ~L\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n  Where’s the answer key?\\r\\n  \\r\\nYou’ll find the answer key at the end of the chapter on page 148.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]
