Choose a fact to explore.\'}>\\r\\n      <rect x=\\"28\\" y=\\"25\\" width=\\"584\\" height=\\"275\\" rx=\\"28\\" className=\\"co-packed\\"/><text x=\\"54\\" y=\\"63\\">Packed orders</text><rect x=\\"70\\" y=\\"99\\" width=\\"240\\" height=\\"164\\" rx=\\"24\\" className=\\"co-ready\\"/><text x=\\"94\\" y=\\"131\\">Marked ready</text>{placed && <g className=\\"co-animal\\" style={{ transform: `translate(${x}px,${placed ? 198 : 125}px)` }}><circle r=\\"35\\"/><text textAnchor=\\"middle\\" y=\\"6\\">Order {unknown ? \'B\' : \'A\'}</text></g>}\\r\\n    </svg><div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button aria-pressed={fact === \'ready\'} onClick={() => { setFact(\'ready\'); setPlaced(false); }}>Order A is marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={fact === \'packed\'} onClick={() => { setFact(\'packed\'); setPlaced(false); setPossibility(\'ready\'); }}>Order B has been packed</button><button onClick={() => setPlaced(true)}>Place the order</button></div>\\r\\n    {placed && unknown && <div className=\\"co-choices\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Allowed placements, not established facts\\"><button aria-pressed={possibility === \'ready\'} onClick={() => setPossibility(\'ready\')}>Could be marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={possibility === \'not-ready\'} onClick={() => setPossibility(\'not-ready\')}>Could be packed without a ready label</button></div>}\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-feedback\\" role=\\"status\\">{!placed ? \'Choose a fact, then use the rule to see what we know.\' : !unknown ? \'Order A is marked ready, so it must have been packed. The ready label gives us packing.\' : \'Order B has been packed. Whether it is marked ready remains unknown. Both placements fit the rule; neither has been established.\'}</p><p className=\\"co-small\\">The rule requires every ready order to be packed. It does not require more packed orders than ready orders. This drawing shows an allowed arrangement.</p></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction GateExplorer() {\\r\\n  const [fact, setFact] = useState<\'ready\' | \'packed\'>(\'ready\');\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-gate\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Enough and required</p><Arrow left=\\"Marked ready\\" right=\\"Packed\\"/><div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button aria-pressed={fact === \'ready\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'ready\')}>Order A is marked ready</button><button aria-pressed={fact === \'packed\'} onClick={() => setFact(\'packed\')}>Order B has been packed</button></div><div className=\\"co-gate-track\\"><div className={fact === \'ready\' ? \'co-lit\' : \'\'}>Marked ready<span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</b><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">implies</span><div className=\\"co-lit\\">Packed<span>Necessary: required</span></div></div><p role=\\"status\\">{fact === \'ready\' ? \'We know Order A has been packed because every ready order is packed.\' : \'We know Order B has been packed. Its ready status remains unknown. An order can be packed without a ready label.\'}</p></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\ntype Translation = { word: string; sentence: string; target: string; other: string; left: string; right: string; why: string; readback: string };\\r\\ntype MappedTranslation = Translation & { sentenceTarget: string };\\r\\ntype DiagramTranslation = MappedTranslation & { reversedPromise: string };\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function orderedConditionalIdeas(item: Pick<Translation, \'sentence\' | \'target\' | \'other\'>): [string, string] {\\r\\n  let hash = 2166136261;\\r\\n  for (const character of item.sentence) hash = Math.imul(hash ^ character.charCodeAt(0), 16777619);\\r\\n  const reverseOrder = ((hash ^ (hash >>> 16)) >>> 0) % 2 === 1;\\r\\n  return reverseOrder ? [item.other, item.target] : [item.target, item.other];\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const translationExamples: Record<string, MappedTranslation[]> = {\\r\\n  \'group-1\': [\\r\\n    { word: \'if\', sentence: \'A report is reviewed if it is submitted before noon.\', sentenceTarget: \'it is submitted before noon\', target: \'Submitted before noon\', other: \'Reviewed\', left: \'Submitted before noon\', right: \'Reviewed\', why: \'If what? Submission before noon. The sufficient clause comes second, but it still goes left.\', readback: \'If a report is submitted before noon, then it is reviewed.\' },\\r\\n    { word: \'when\', sentence: \'When the alarm sounds, the staff evacuate.\', sentenceTarget: \'the alarm sounds\', target: \'Alarm sounds\', other: \'Staff evacuate\', left: \'Alarm sounds\', right: \'Staff evacuate\', why: \'When what? The alarm sounds. The rule says every alarm brings an evacuation.\', readback: \'If the alarm sounds, then the staff evacuate.\' },\\r\\n    { word: \'where\', sentence: \'Where a station is available, the service operates.\', sentenceTarget: \'a station is available\', target: \'Station available\', other: \'Service operates\', left: \'Station available\', right: \'Service operates\', why: \'Where what? A station is available. This rule makes station availability enough for service.\', readback: \'If a station is available at a location, then the service operates there.\' },\\r\\n    { word: \'all\', sentence: \'All registered guests receive a wristband.\', sentenceTarget: \'registered guests\', target: \'Registered guest\', other: \'Receives wristband\', left: \'Registered guest\', right: \'Receives wristband\', why: \'…1989 tokens truncated…ne doesn\'t put a mug in the blue display.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"A lidded mug from another display would fit this rule. Your arrow would put that mug in the blue display. The sentence tells us what blue-display mugs come with; it hasn\'t restricted lids to them.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"the only\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"The only recipes printed in this booklet are vegetarian recipes.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"recipes printed in this booklet\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Recipe printed in booklet\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Vegetarian recipe\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Recipe printed in booklet\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Vegetarian recipe\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If a recipe is printed in this booklet, then it is vegetarian.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"Pick a recipe printed in this booklet. The sentence tells you it is vegetarian. It hasn\'t promised a place in the booklet to every vegetarian recipe, so being printed there is the starting condition.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"A vegetarian recipe could be missing from the booklet. Nothing here requires every vegetarian recipe to be printed. Your reverse arrow adds that promise of a place in the booklet.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"every\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"Every jacket stored in the front closet has a hanger.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"jacket stored in the front closet\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Jacket stored in front closet\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Has hanger\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Jacket stored in front closet\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Has hanger\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If a jacket is stored in the front closet, then it has a hanger.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"The front-closet location is part of the condition. A jacket stored there has a hanger. Seeing a jacket on a hanger doesn\'t tell you where it is stored.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"A jacket hanging in the back closet doesn\'t break this rule. A hanger proves neither closet. Starting from the hanger would make the front-closet location certain without that connection.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"any\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"Any sandwich ordered without cheese gets a green wrapper.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"sandwich ordered without cheese\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Sandwich ordered without cheese\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Gets green wrapper\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Sandwich ordered without cheese\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Gets green wrapper\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If a sandwich is ordered without cheese, then it gets a green wrapper.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"The rule concerns sandwiches ordered without cheese. Those orders get green wrappers. Keep without cheese with the sandwich; a green wrapper alone doesn\'t tell us whether the sandwich was ordered without cheese.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"A sandwich ordered with cheese could also get a green wrapper. The sentence hasn\'t reserved those wrappers for cheese-free orders. Your arrow makes that restriction even though the original doesn\'t.\\"\\r\\n    }\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \\"group-2\\": [\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"only\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"Only guests with a booking receive a room key.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"guests with a booking\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Guest has booking\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Receives room key\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Receives room key\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Guest has booking\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If a guest receives a room key, then that guest has a booking.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"A room key tells you the guest has a booking because only limits keys to those guests. Nothing says every guest with a booking receives a key, so a booking alone leaves that question open.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"A guest could have a booking without receiving a key. That meets the requirement without establishing the result. Booking \\\\u2192 Receives key would promise a key to every booked guest.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"only if\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"A printer releases a document only if its job has been approved.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"its job has been approved\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Job approved\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Printer releases document\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Printer releases document\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Job approved\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If a printer releases a document, then its job has been approved.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"The printer needs an approved job to release the document. Release therefore establishes approval. A job that is approved but unreleased would fit the sentence too.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"An approved job could remain unreleased. The rule requires approval for release; it doesn\'t guarantee release for every approved job. Your arrow would make approval enough.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"only when\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentence\\": \\"The fountain runs only when its pump is switched on.\\",\\r\\n      \\"sentenceTarget\\": \\"its pump is switched on\\",\\r\\n      \\"target\\": \\"Pump switched on\\",\\r\\n      \\"other\\": \\"Fountain runs\\",\\r\\n      \\"left\\": \\"Fountain runs\\",\\r\\n      \\"right\\": \\"Pump switched on\\",\\r\\n      \\"readback\\": \\"If the fountain runs, then its pump is switched on.\\",\\r\\n      \\"why\\": \\"If the fountain is running, its pump is switched on. That\'s what only when requires. The pump could be on while the fountain isn\'t running; nothing in the sentence rules that out.\\",\\r\\n      \\"reversedPromise\\": \\"The pump could be on while the fountain isn\'t running. That fits the stated requirement. Your arrow would exclude it by turning the pump\'s switched-on state into a guarantee of running.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"word\\": \\"only where\\",\\r\\n"}}\n{"i":2,"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"38e5e9","wall_time_seconds":0.5875993,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3412,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}}\n{"i":3,"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"af238f","wall_time_seconds":0.4276595,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":0,"output":""}}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"3752f2","wall_time_seconds":0.223663,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6005,"output":"          \\"You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn\'t reported that anyone did it.\\",\\r\\n          \\"The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.\\",\\r\\n          \\"The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn\'t say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.\\",\\r\\n        ]}/>\\r\\n        <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn\'t used, and told to return isn\'t returned.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n    </>}\\r\\n    {roles && <>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Sufficient means enough\\">\\r\\n        <p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know an order has been packed. That makes the ready label <strong>sufficient</strong> for packing. Sufficient means enough.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The rule already tells you what has to be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>\\r\\n          <p>This doesn\'t make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Enough does not mean the only way\\">\\r\\n        <p>Suppose Order D isn\'t marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn\'t settle the packing question.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order D isn\'t marked ready. Could it still have been packed?\\">\\r\\n          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn\'t been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>\\r\\n          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn\'t said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn\'t present, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?\\" options={[\'Marked collected\', \'Has a pickup receipt\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.\', \'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.\']}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Necessary means required\\">\\r\\n        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready for pickup has to have been packed. Packing is required for an order with that label. That makes being packed <strong>necessary</strong> for being marked ready.</p>\\r\\n        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Which condition does a ready order have to meet?\\">\\r\\n          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we\'d have a ready order that wasn\'t packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>\\r\\n          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>\\r\\n        <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Enters the archive\\" right=\\"Has a pass\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?\\" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', \\"You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too.\\"]}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?\\">\\r\\n          <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. My order could be packed without a ready label. That fits every ready order is packed because it doesn\'t create a ready order that hasn\'t been packed.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Packed → Marked ready would add a claim about every packed order. The sentence doesn\'t give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Read the roles in the relationship\\">\\r\\n        <p>In <strong>Marked ready → Packed</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it\'s enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it\'s required whenever the left holds.</p>\\r\\n        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don\'t ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n    </>}\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === \'group-1\' ? \'g1\' : \'g2\'))!;\\r\\n  const first = id === \'group-1\';\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the sufficient idea\' : \'Find what is required\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We\'ll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-vocabulary\\">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. Finding that idea matters more than taking the next few words in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <IndicatorSections id={id}/>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the target, then place it\' : \'Find the requirement, then place it\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Add what to the indicator when that gives you a sensible question. If what? All what? The only what? Your answer should be the whole idea the expression refers to. Then check whether that idea really is enough to establish the other condition.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"Ask what the indicator refers to. Only what? Only if what? What is required? The target can come before or after the expression, so check its meaning in the complete sentence.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <p>This is the <strong>What Test</strong>. The question helps you find the indicator\'s complete target before choosing its side of the arrow. Keep any words that limit which people, objects, places, or occasions the sentence covers. The question finds the idea; the whole sentence tells you what that idea does.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Work through the expressions below. First choose the complete target. Then place it on its side of the arrow and read the relationship in ordinary English.</p>\\r\\n      <TranslationLab id={id}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Keep the whole condition\\" dark>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{first ? \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.\' : \'Entry requires a current pass.\'}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Keep that restriction when you shorten the idea; otherwise your diagram would make a claim about on-time applicants too.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. Shortening a label should save writing while preserving what the sentence requires.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <Check prompt={first ? \'What goes left in this rule?\' : \'What goes right in this rule?\'} options={first ? [\'Applicant\', \'Applicant submits late\'] : [\'Has current pass\', \'Has any pass\']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first\\r\\n        ? [\'You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.\', \'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.\']\\r\\n        : [\'This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.\', \'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the sentence requires.\']}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Read the only as a complete expression\' : \'Compare only with the only\'}>\\r\\n      {first ? <>\\r\\n        <blockquote>The only people admitted are members.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The only what? The only people admitted. The complete target is admitted people. If someone is admitted, the sentence tells us they\'re a member.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Admitted\\" right=\\"Member\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>A member who stays outside fits this rule. Nothing says every member is admitted. Check that your diagram keeps that possibility open.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"The only notebooks kept on this shelf are hardback. Which diagram preserves the rule?\\" options={[\'Hardback → Kept on shelf\', \'Kept on shelf → Hardback\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You made being hardback enough for a place on the shelf. The sentence limits the notebooks kept there; it does not put every hardback notebook on this shelf.\', \'A notebook kept on this shelf has to be hardback. A hardback notebook on another shelf would also fit the rule, so this direction keeps what the sentence actually says.\']}/>\\r\\n      </> : <>\\r\\n        <p>Try the distinction on the order rule we already understand. The wording changes while the connection stays the same.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Only packed orders are marked ready.</blockquote><p>Only refers to packed orders. Packing is required for the ready label, so packed goes right.</p></div><div><blockquote>The only orders marked ready are packed orders.</blockquote><p>The only refers to orders marked ready. That label is enough to establish packing, so marked ready goes left.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Marked ready\\" right=\\"Packed\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>Both sentences preserve the same direction. An order that is packed but not marked ready fits both. Neither sentence makes packing alone enough for the label.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Only registered guests receive wristbands. What follows from receiving a wristband?\\" options={[\'The person is a registered guest.\', \'Nothing about registration.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Only limits wristbands to registered guests, so receiving one establishes that the person is registered.\', \'You treated the sentence like all registered guests receive wristbands. Only restricts who receives one, so the wristband does establish that the person is a registered guest.\']}/>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"From sentence to diagram\\">\\r\\n      <p>You have seen the expressions with help. Now use a new sentence. Identify both complete ideas, choose the sufficient one for the left, and check whether your diagram says what the sentence says.</p>\\r\\n      <p>If you know the meaning but hesitate over the arrow, imagine the left condition is true. Does the sentence then require the right condition? Read the whole relationship in English. If it makes a promise the original didn\'t make, change the direction.</p>\\r\\n      <DiagramBuilder id={id}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"co-practical-note\\"><summary>Why write a diagram when I understand the sentence?</summary><p>For one short sentence, you may be able to hold the connection in your head. Here, writing it makes your direction and complete conditions visible so you can check them. If your first diagram doesn\'t match the meaning, find the exact phrase you misread and revise that part.</p></details>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Say the rule without the scaffold\\">\\r\\n      <p>Read the new sentence and explain its direction before revealing. If you hesitate, ask what the indicator refers to, then try the proposed relationship in ordinary English. Compare your explanation with the one that appears.</p>\\r\\n      <Flashcards id={id}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Check the meaning, not just the word\\">\\r\\n      <Check prompt={first ? \'A report is reviewed if submitted before noon. Which is the same rule?\' : \'A student graduates only if the project is completed. Which is the same rule?\'} options={first ? [\'If reviewed, it was submitted before noon.\', \'If submitted before noon, it is reviewed.\'] : [\'If the student graduates, the project was completed.\', \'If the project is completed, the student graduates.\']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first\\r\\n        ? [\'You reversed the if relationship. Submission before noon is enough for review; the sentence has not said review proves when the report was submitted.\', \'This preserves the if-clause as the sufficient condition. Submission before noon gives us review even though it appears second in the original sentence.\']\\r\\n        : [\'This preserves project completion as a requirement for graduation. It does not turn completing the project into a promise of graduation.\', \'You turned the only-if requirement into a guarantee. Graduation requires completion, but the sentence allows a completed project without graduation.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. Then the attached drill gives you fresh questions. Use the explanation to inspect the direction and complete conditions, not only whether you chose the same answer.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction OpeningFigure({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {\\r\\n  if (unitId === \'orientation\') return <figure className=\\"co-hero-figure co-meaning-figure\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">A connection between conditions</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-hero-sentence\\">What would we know <strong>if</strong> this were true?</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-hypothetical-tag\\">A situation to imagine</div>\\r\\n    <figcaption>A conditional gives you a connection. It doesn\'t claim the imagined situation really happened.</figcaption>\\r\\n  </figure>;\\r\\n  if (unitId === \'sufficient-necessary\') return <figure className=\\"co-hero-figure co-roles-figure\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Two views of one rule</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-hero-rule\\">If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-role-compass\\"><div><span>Enough</span><strong>Marked ready</strong><small>Gives us packing</small></div><div><span>Required</span><strong>Packed</strong><small>Every ready order needs it</small></div></div>\\r\\n    <figcaption>One condition is enough. The other is required by that same rule.</figcaption>\\r\\n  </figure>;\\r\\n  if (unitId === \'group-1\') return <figure className=\\"co-hero-figure co-sufficient-figure\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Read the complete idea</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-hero-sentence\\">A report is reviewed <mark>if</mark> <strong>it is submitted before noon.</strong></p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-hero-target\\"><span>What is enough for review?</span><strong>Submission before noon</strong></div>\\r\\n    <figcaption>The sufficient condition can come last in the sentence.</figcaption>\\r\\n  </figure>;\\r\\n  return <figure className=\\"co-hero-figure co-required-figure\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Read what the sentence needs</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-hero-sentence\\">Entry requires a <strong>current pass.</strong></p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-pass-figure\\"><span>Required for entry</span><strong>A current pass</strong><small>An expired pass won\'t meet this requirement.</small></div>\\r\\n    <figcaption>Having the pass alone doesn\'t establish entry.</figcaption>\\r\\n  </figure>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalOpeningLesson({ unit, onComplete, continueHref, continueLabel = \'Continue to guided practice\', previousHref, reviewed }: ConditionalLessonNoteProps) {\\r\\n  const [motionPaused, setMotionPaused] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const [activeInteraction, setActiveInteraction] = useState<HTMLElement | null>(null);\\r\\n  const [tocHidden, setTocHidden] = useState(false);\\r\\n  useEffect(() => {\\r\\n    if (!activeInteraction) { setTocHidden(false); return; }\\r\\n    setTocHidden(true);\\r\\n    if (typeof IntersectionObserver === \'undefined\') return;\\r\\n    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => setTocHidden(entry.isIntersecting));\\r\\n    observer.observe(activeInteraction);\\r\\n    return () => observer.disconnect();\\r\\n  }, [activeInteraction]);\\r\\n  const protectInteraction = (target: EventTarget) => {\\r\\n    if (!(target instanceof HTMLElement)) return;\\r\\n    const interaction = target.closest<HTMLElement>(\'.co-lab,.co-think-pause,.co-flash,.co-check\');\\r\\n    if (interaction) { setTocHidden(true); setActiveInteraction(interaction); }\\r\\n    else if (target.closest(\'button,a\')) setActiveInteraction(null);\\r\\n  };\\r\\n  const group = unit.id === \'group-1\' || unit.id === \'group-2\';\\r\\n  const orientation = unit.id === \'orientation\';\\r\\n  const title = group ? unit.id === \'group-1\' ? \'Group 1: what is enough?\' : \'Group 2: what is required?\' : orientation ? \'What Are Conditionals?\' : \'What is enough? What is required?\';\\r\\n  const repairTitle = orientation ? \\"Don\'t turn a rule into a case fact\\" : unit.id === \'sufficient-necessary\' ? \\"Don\'t turn a requirement into a guarantee\\" : unit.id === \'group-1\' ? \'Keep the direction and the whole condition\' : \'Keep requirements and completed actions distinct\';\\r\\n  const subtitle = orientation\\r\\n    ? \\"A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too. Let\'s see what that connection lets you conclude, and what it leaves open.\\"\\r\\n    : group ? unit.id === \'group-1\'\\r\\n      ? \'These expressions can point to the sufficient idea. Read what each one says before deciding which complete condition belongs on the left.\'\\r\\n      : \'These expressions can identify a requirement. Find the complete idea the sentence says you need, then check what its presence does and does not prove.\'\\r\\n    : \\"The same rule gives its two conditions different jobs. Let\'s take each one seriously before giving it a place in the diagram.\\";\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"conditional-note co-opening\\" data-motion-paused={motionPaused} onFocusCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)} onClickCapture={event => protectInteraction(event.target)}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-lesson-navigation\\" hidden={tocHidden}><DynamicIslandTOC key={unit.id} selector=\\".co-opening [data-toc]\\"/></div>\\r\\n    <header className={\'co-hero \' + (orientation ? \'co-intro-hero\' : \'\')}>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-hero-copy\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Conditional logic</p><h1>{title}</h1><p>{subtitle}</p>\\r\\n      <a href=\\"#co-reading\\">{orientation ? \'Start with the meaning\' : \'Begin reading\'} <span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">↓</span></a></div>\\r\\n      <OpeningFigure unitId={unit.id}/>\\r\\n    </header>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-reading-preferences\\"><button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-motion-toggle\\" aria-pressed={motionPaused} onClick={() => setMotionPaused(!motionPaused)}>Reduce animation in this lesson</button><span>The same setting applies to the explanations, diagrams, and cards.</span></div>\\r\\n    <article id=\\"co-reading\\">\\r\\n      {group ? <GroupLessons id={unit.id}/> : <Fundamentals roles={unit.id === \'sufficient-necessary\'}/>}\\r\\n      <ConditionalOpeningMedia unitId={unit.id}/>\\r\\n      <Section id=\\"repair\\" title={repairTitle}>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><h3>The tempting move</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.mistake}</p></div><div><h3>Read the sentence again</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.repair}</p></div></div>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <footer className=\\"co-footer\\">\\r\\n        <p>{reviewed ? \'You marked this lesson as read. You can return to the examples whenever you need.\' : \'Marking this lesson as read records review. Your practice gives you a chance to use the reasoning yourself.\'}</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-choices\\">{previousHref && <Link to={previousHref}>Previous lesson</Link>}<button type=\\"button\\" onClick={onComplete}>{reviewed ? \'Lesson read\' : \'Mark lesson as read\'}</button><Link className=\\"co-primary\\" to={continueHref}>{continueLabel} <span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span></Link></div>\\r\\n      </footer>\\r\\n    </article>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-conditional-bootcamp/src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx\n+import { useEffect, useId, useRef, useState, type ReactNode } from 'react';\n+import './reference-sequence.css';\n+\n+const orderRule = 'If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.';\n+\n+function ReferenceStep({ number, title, label, children, dark = false }: { number: number; title: string; label: string; children: ReactNode; dark?: boolean }) {\n+  return <section id={`reference-step-${number}`} data-reference-step={number} data-toc data-toc-title={label} className={`cr-step co-section ${dark ? 'cr-dark' : ''}`}>\n+    <header className=\"cr-step-heading\"><span className=\"cr-number\" aria-hidden=\"true\">{String(number).padStart(2, '0')}</span><div><p className=\"co-kicker\">{label}</p><h2>{title}</h2></div></header>\n+    <div className=\"cr-step-body\">{children}</div>\n+  </section>;\n+}\n+\n+function ReferenceQuestion({ prompt, options, correct, feedback }: { prompt: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] }) {\n+  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<number | null>(null);\n+  const id = useId();\n+  return <div className=\"cr-question co-check\"><h3 id={id}>{prompt}</h3><div className=\"cr-answer-options\" role=\"group\" aria-labelledby={id}>\n+    {options.map((option, index) => <button type=\"button\" key={option} aria-pressed={choice === index} onClick={() => setChoice(index)}>{option}</button>)}\n+  </div><div role=\"status\" aria-live=\"polite\">{choice !== null && <div className={`cr-feedback ${choice === correct ? 'cr-correct' : 'cr-revise'}`}><strong>{choice === correct ? For a person in that group, having a key follows.' },\n+  { word: 'each', sentence: 'Each person who borrows a locker has a key.', explanation: 'Each applies the statement to every individual locker borrower. We have changed the wording, not what borrowing establishes.' },\n+];\n+const necessaryFamily: FamilyEntry[] = [\n+  { word: 'then', sentence: 'If someone borrows a locker, then they have a key.', explanation: 'Then introduces what the borrowing condition establishes: the person has a key.' },\n+  { word: 'must', sentence: 'A person borrowing a locker must have a key.', explanation: 'Here we are describing a requirement under the stated rule. If must appears in an instruction instead, preserve that meaning: a required action is not proof that someone completed it.' },\n+  { word: 'necessary', sentence: 'Having a key is necessary for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'Ask necessary for what? For borrowing a locker. Having a key is the requirement, not a promise that someone borrowed.' },\n+  { word: 'required', sentence: 'Having a key is required for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'Required names the same relationship. Only people with a key borrow a locker says the same thing. We'll keep the same locker relationship while reading each one.</p> : <><p className=\"co-kicker\">{entries[selected].word}</p><blockquote>{entries[selected].sentence}</blockquote><p>{entries[selected].explanation}</p><div className=\"cr-family-role\"><span>{role === 'sufficient' ? 'cr-correct' : 'cr-revise'}`}><strong>{correct ? 'Both diagrams are correct.' : 'Review the relationship.'}</strong><p>{original === item.originalPosition ? 'Your original preserves the direction.' : 'Your original changes the direction or negates conditions the sentence did not negate.'} {contra === item.contraPosition ? 'Your contrapositive reverses and negates both complete conditions.' : 'Your contrapositive must start from the missing requirement and rule out the sufficient condition.'}</p><p>{item.why}</p><p className=\"cr-answer-key\">Original: {item.leftCode} → {item.rightCode}<br/>Contrapositive: ~{item.rightCode} → ~{item.leftCode}</p><p>Read it back: if {item.left.toLowerCase()}, then {item.right.toLowerCase()}. If the required condition is absent, the sufficient condition cannot hold.</p></div>}</div>\n+  </div>;\n+}\n+\n+export function ConditionalReferenceSequence() {\n+  return <div className=\"cr-sequence\">\n+    <ReferenceStep number={1} label=\"Start with meaning\" title=\"What does a conditional tell you?\">\n+      <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is an idea that can be true or false. An order has been packed. A guest has a ticket. We can ask whether either condition holds in a particular case.</p>\n+      <p>A <strong>conditional</strong> connects two conditions. It says that if the first is true, the other has to be true too.</p>\n+      <p className=\"co-if-inline\">We don't need to know whether the first condition really occurred to understand the connection. We're asking what would follow <strong>if</strong> it did.</p>\n+      <HypotheticalFigure/>\n+      <h3>Why learn this?</h3><p>On the LSAT, an argument may give you a rule and ask what follows from it. Or it may treat one fact as enough to prove another. Understanding that connection helps you decide what the argument establishes and where it goes further than its information allows.</p>\n+      <p>Let's read one rule.</p><blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote>\n+      <p>For this rule, the ready label makes packing certain. A ready order that hasn't been packed would break the stated relationship. That's what we mean by a <strong>guarantee</strong> here.</p>\n+      <p>Compare that with “Ready orders are usually packed.” Usually allows an exception. Our if/then rule doesn't. We are working with the rule as given; we aren't claiming that every shop in real life follows it.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Does the rule itself tell us that an actual order is marked ready?\" options={['Yes, at least one order is marked ready.', 'No, it tells us what would follow if an order were marked ready.']} correct={1} feedback={['You added a case fact. The rule connects the conditions but has not reported an order with that label.', 'The imagined ready order lets us understand the rule. We need a separate fact to establish that an actual order has the label.']}/>\n+      <p>Now let's take the two conditions one at a time.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={2} label=\"Sufficient means enough\" title=\"One condition gives you enough\">\n+      <blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote><p>The <strong>if-part</strong> is the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Sufficient means enough. Knowing an order is marked ready is enough, under this rule, to know it has been packed.</p>\n+      <div className=\"cr-role-exhibit\"><span>Sufficient</span><strong>Marked ready for pickup</strong><p>Enough to establish packing</p></div>\n+      <p>The ready label might be present. It might not be. The rule hasn't settled that question for every order.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Order D isn't marked ready. Could it still have been packed?\" options={['Yes. Packing is still possible.', 'No. Without the label, packing is impossible.']} correct={0} feedback={['D could be packed without the label. It could also be unpacked. Neither case creates a ready order that is unpacked, so the rule leaves packing open.', 'You made the ready label the only way to have packing. The sentence gives one condition that establishes packing; it has not excluded other packed orders.']}/>\n+      <p>Now suppose a fact gives us the sufficient condition. That is when we can <strong>use</strong>, or activate, the rule in its stated direction.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Order E is marked ready. Do we need another fact to establish packing?\" options={['Yes, we need an additional packing fact.', 'No, the rule and the ready label establish it.']} correct={1} feedback={['The rule already covers every ready order, including E. The separate ready-label fact meets its sufficient condition.', 'E meets the sufficient condition. With the rule, that is enough to establish that E has been packed.']}/>\n+      <p>We'll now keep this job in mind while changing the wording.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={3} label=\"The sufficient family\" title=\"Different words can give you the same connection\">\n+      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps you recognize a conditional relationship. These expressions can introduce an idea that is enough to establish another.</p>\n+      <p>For the next examples, use a different stated rule: anyone who borrows a locker has a key. We will express that same connection in several ways. We haven't said everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\n+      <WordFamily entries={sufficientFamily} role=\"sufficient\"/>\n+      <p>When/whenever and any/anytime group related forms together. An ending changes the kind of case being discussed, but these examples keep the same connection. Read the whole sentence. A familiar word is a clue, not a substitute for its meaning.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Every guest wearing a blue badge receives a lunch voucher. Which complete idea is enough?\" options={['Guest', 'Guest wearing a blue badge', 'Receives a lunch voucher']} correct={1} feedback={['Guest alone drops the badge restriction and would make a claim about unbadged guests.', 'The rule gives a voucher to every guest wearing a blue badge. Keep that qualification with the person.', 'The voucher is what the rule establishes. It has not said every voucher recipient wears a blue badge.']}/>\n+      <details className=\"cr-extension\"><summary>Two more Group 1 expressions</summary><p><strong>Where</strong> can state a relationship across locations: “Where a collection point operates, parcels can be returned.” An operating collection point establishes the availability of returns there. Availability is not proof that someone made a return.</p><p><strong>The only</strong> needs its complete phrase: “The only notebooks on this shelf are hardback.” A notebook on the shelf is hardback. The sentence does not put every hardback notebook there.</p><p>These are part of your Group 1 work. Understand what the full expression covers before using its group as a placement shortcut.</p></details>\n+      <p>We have seen what can be enough. Now let's name what that condition requires.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={4} label=\"Necessary means required\" title=\"The other condition has to be there\">\n+      <blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote><p>The <strong>then-part</strong> is the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. A ready order has to have been packed.</p>\n+      <div className=\"cr-role-exhibit cr-required\"><span>Necessary</span><strong>Has been packed</strong><p>Required for an order marked ready</p></div>\n+      <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order with the ready label. These names describe the roles the conditions have in this relationship.</p>\n+      <p>We're looking at the same connection from its other side. The ready label gives us enough to establish packing. Packing is what every ready order requires.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Under our rule, which condition must a ready order meet?\" options={['It has been packed.', 'It has been collected by the customer.']} correct={0} feedback={['Packing is the stated requirement. A ready order that was not packed would violate the rule.', 'Collection is not part of this rule. The sentence establishes packing, not whether the customer collected the order.']}/>\n+      <p>Necessary does not mean “the next event.” It means required by this relationship. A conditional need not tell you which event caused another, or when either happened.</p>\n+      <p>As with sufficient language, the wording can vary while the relationship stays the same.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={5} label=\"The necessary family\" title=\"Listen for what the sentence needs\">\n+      <p>Return to our locker rule. Borrowing a locker requires a key. The next expressions all identify that requirement in their complete sentences.</p>\n+      <WordFamily entries={necessaryFamily} role=\"necessary\"/>\n+      <div className=\"cr-contrast\"><div><h3>If</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is enough for borrowing under this sentence.</p></div><div><h3>Only if</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>only if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is required. This sentence has not made it enough.</p></div></div>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"A guest enters the lounge only if the guest has a current pass. What does entering establish?\" options={['The guest has a current pass.', 'Every guest with a pass enters.']} correct={0} feedback={['Only if identifies the current pass as required for entry. An entering guest therefore meets that requirement.', 'You made the requirement enough. A guest could have a current pass and remain outside without violating the sentence.']}/>\n+      <details className=\"cr-extension\"><summary>More Group 2 forms, with the sentence intact</summary><p><strong>Only when</strong> and <strong>only where</strong> can restrict the occasions or places of an outcome: “The fountain runs only when its pump is on.” A running fountain requires an operating pump. The pump being on does not establish that the fountain runs.</p><p><strong>Requires</strong> directly names a requirement: “Entry requires a current pass.” Preserve current. <strong>Always</strong> needs the sentence's subject and scope: “The upstairs room is always quiet when the door is shut.” Here a shut door establishes quiet, not the other way around.</p><p><strong>Only</strong> and <strong>the only</strong> are different complete expressions. “Only packed orders are marked ready” and “The only orders marked ready are packed” both tell us that a ready order is packed.</p></details>\n+      <p>We can now recognize both roles before writing any symbols. Next, let's see what happens when a requirement is missing.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={6} label=\"Discover the contrapositive\" title=\"What if the requirement is missing?\" dark>\n+      <p>Use this stated rule for a different case.</p><blockquote>If a report is in the published archive, it has an approval stamp.</blockquote>\n+      <p>A report in that archive has to carry the stamp. Now you inspect Report K. It has no approval stamp.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Could Report K be in the published archive while this rule holds?\" options={['Yes, its location is unrestricted.', 'No, it cannot be in that archive.', 'Yes, the missing stamp puts it in the archive.']} correct={1} feedback={['Its location is restricted by the rule. If K were in the archive, it would have a stamp, which conflicts with the given fact.', 'An archived K would have to have a stamp. K has no stamp, so it cannot be in the published archive under this rule.', 'The missing stamp rules out archive membership. It does not establish it.']}/>\n+      <p>That way of using the rule is the <strong>contrapositive</strong>: when the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition cannot be present.</p>\n+      <p>It is the same relationship. We didn't add a second rule; we worked out a consequence already contained in the first one.</p>\n+      <blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"An order has not been packed. What follows about its ready label?\" options={['It is not marked ready for pickup.', 'It is marked ready for pickup.', 'Nothing follows about the ready label.']} correct={0} feedback={['A ready label would require packing. Since packing is absent, the order cannot have that label under the rule.', 'A ready but unpacked order would break the rule. The absent requirement excludes the ready label.', 'The missing requirement does give an inference. If this order were ready, it would have to be packed.']}/>\n+      <p>Don't confuse that with starting from a requirement that is present. A packed order could still be waiting for its ready label. Let's compare all four starting facts.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={7} label=\"The four situations\" title=\"Keep what follows separate from what stays open\">\n+      <p>Change the fact about one order while keeping the rule. Each fact gives a different starting point. Decide what it establishes before comparing the explanation.</p>\n+      <FourCaseExplorer/>\n+      <p>Starting from packing and concluding ready is the tempting <strong>reverse</strong>. Starting from not ready and concluding not packed is the tempting <strong>inverse</strong>. Neither follows from this rule alone.</p>\n+      <p>The contrapositive starts from <strong>not packed</strong> and establishes <strong>not ready</strong>. Its reasoning depends on the missing requirement, not on reversing whichever words happen to appear.</p>\n+      <p>We now understand the relationship in words. Symbols will let us record that understanding without carrying every word of the sentence.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\"Build the first diagram\" title=\"Give the relationship a shorter form\">\n+      <p>Put the sufficient condition on the <strong>left</strong>, the necessary condition on the <strong>right</strong>, and a <strong>must arrow</strong> between them.</p>\n+      <RuleDiagram left=\"Sufficient: enough\" right=\"Necessary: required\"/>\n+      <p>The arrow means the left condition requires the right. It does not mean the left caused the right, or that the right happens later.</p>\n+      <p>Both word families help us choose a condition's role. “If” can introduce what goes left. “Only if” can introduce what goes right. Sentence position alone does not choose the side.</p>\n+      <p>Let's build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\n+      <IncrementalWriter/>\n+      <p>Now move the if-clause: “An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup.” The ready condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job hasn't changed.</p>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\" options={['Packed → Ready', 'Ready → Packed']} correct={1} feedback={['You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes being marked ready enough for packing.', 'The sufficient condition is still marked ready. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.']}/>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\"Write and read the contrapositive\" title=\"Write the meaning you already understand\">\n+      <p>Ready means the order is marked ready for pickup. Packed means it has been packed. Our original is Ready → Packed.</p>\n+      <p>We already know that a missing packing requirement rules out the ready label. Write that as <strong>~Packed → ~Ready</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>.</p>\n+      <p>To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate <strong>both complete conditions</strong>. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let's try all four patterns.</p>\n+      <SignPatterns/>\n+      <div className=\"cr-method\"><p className=\"co-kicker\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\n+      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn't copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\n+      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition's absence and work back to the left condition's absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\"Practical diagramming questions\" title=\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\">\n+      <div className=\"cr-faq\">\n+        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\n+        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\n+        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. Your teacher can add timing to medium or advanced practice after the reasoning is stable.</p></details>\n+        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\n+        <aside className=\"cr-training-note\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We're checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\n+        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. R might mean marked ready for pickup. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you're tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\n+      </div>\n+      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\" options={['The labels need to be longer.', 'The arrow makes the requirement enough.', 'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.']} correct={1} feedback={['Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.', 'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.', 'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.']}/>\n+      <p>That error came from the language. Let's inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\"Find the target\" title=\"Read the whole idea the indicator refers to\">\n+      <p>An indicator's <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\n+      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn't always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\n+      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\n+      <div className=\"cr-language-pair\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\n+      <p>Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Read which complete idea the sentence calls required. Then give that idea its role in the diagram.</p>\n+      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\"Check an interpretation\" title=\"Try the sentence from the inside\">\n+      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\n+      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\n+      <div className=\"cr-personalized\"><div><p className=\"co-kicker\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"If I'm riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\" options={['I am wearing a helmet.', 'The helmet question is open.']} correct={0} feedback={['The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.', 'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.']}/></div><div><p className=\"co-kicker\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\"If I'm wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\" options={['Yes, the helmet proves riding.', 'No, I could wear one without riding.']} correct={1} feedback={['You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.', 'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.']}/></div></div>\n+      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\n+      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Ask a sensible question about the expression: only what? People wearing helmets. What is required? Wearing a helmet. What is enough? Riding a studio bike, under this rule, establishes that requirement.</p>\n+      <p>The What Test finds the complete target. The Personalized If/Then checks what your proposed direction says about a case. Use them together when one part of the translation is unclear.</p>\n+      <p>We've now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+\n+    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\"Use the whole process\" title=\"Build an original and its contrapositive\">\n+      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\n+      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\n+      <div className=\"cr-integrated-grid\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\n+      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\n+    </ReferenceStep>\n+  </div>;\n+}\n*** Add File:
