[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"7af587","wall_time_seconds":0.1722127,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1828,"output":"817-        <Check prompt=\\"Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?\\" options={[\'Yes, the badge proves it.\', \'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.\', \'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.\']}/>\\r\\n818-        <p>A badge may make volunteer status seem plausible. Check whether the sentence actually told you that everyone with a badge is a volunteer. This one didn\'t.</p>\\r\\n819-      </Section>\\r\\n820:      <Section title=\\"Keep what the sentence actually gives you\\">\\r\\n821-        <p>Keep the sentence\'s own strength. A rule can give you permission to do something without saying you did it. It can also tell you what you\'re supposed to do without reporting that you followed the instruction. Those are different claims.</p>\\r\\n822-        <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote>\\r\\n823-        <p>May use gives permission here. A guest with a ticket is allowed to use the cloakroom. That guest could leave their coat on and never go inside. The permission is still there.</p>\\r\\n824:        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\">\\n825-          <p>No. We know she\'s allowed to use it because the rule gives that permission to every guest with a ticket. We haven\'t been told whether she used it.</p>\\r\\n826-          <p>Compare this instruction: <strong>If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack.</strong> A wet coat makes the instruction call for hanging it up. That doesn\'t report anyone carrying out the instruction. We\'d need a fact about what they actually did.</p>\\r\\n827-        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n828-        <Check prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\\r\\n829:          \\"You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn\'t reported that anyone did it.\\",\\r\\n830:          \\"The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.\\",\\r\\n831-          \\"The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn\'t say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.\\",\\r\\n832-        ]}/>\\r\\n833-        <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn\'t used, and told to return isn\'t returned.</p>\\r\\n834-      </Section>\\r\\n835-    </>}\\r\\n836-    {roles && <>\\r\\n837-      <Section title=\\"Sufficient means enough\\">\\r\\n838-        <p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know an order has been packed. That makes the ready label <strong>sufficient</strong> for packing. Sufficient means enough.</p>\\r\\n839-        <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n840-        <p>The rule already tells you what has to be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>\\r\\n841-        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?\\">\\r\\n842-          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>\\r\\n843-          <p>This doesn\'t make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>\\r\\n844-        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n845-      </Section>\\r\\n846-      <Section title=\\"Enough does not mean the only way\\">\\r\\n847-        <p>Suppose Order D isn\'t marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn\'t settle the packing question.</p>\\r\\n848-        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order D isn\'t marked ready. Could it still have been packed?\\">\\r\\n849-          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn\'t been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>\\r\\n850-          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn\'t said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn\'t present, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\r\\n851-        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n852-        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\\r\\n853-        <Check prompt=\\"Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?\\" options={[\'Marked collected\', \'Has a pickup receipt\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.\', \'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.\']}/>\\r\\n854-      </Section>\\r\\n855-      <Section title=\\"Necessary means required\\">\\r\\n856-        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready for pickup has to have been packed. Packing is required for an order with that label. That makes being packed <strong>necessary</strong> for being marked ready.</p>\\r\\n857-        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>\\r\\n858-        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>\\r\\n859-        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Which condition does a ready order have to meet?\\">\\r\\n860-          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we\'d have a ready order that wasn\'t packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>\\r\\n861-          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n862-        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n863-        <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n864-        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>\\r\\n865-        <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n866-      </Section>\\r\\n867-      <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n868-        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n869-        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n870-        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n"}'}]

The notice has not reported that anyone returned this tray.', 'The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. ## assistant

The full sequence is now rendered. I’m tightening two teaching details before the final checks: the missing-requirement explanation will appear after the student’s prediction, and practice labels will vary in order so students have to read the sentence rather than follow a visual shortcut.
